L46: ## 1 Executive Summary
L47: ## 1.1. Product Introduction
L48: Skyrizi (risankizumab) injection for subcutaneous use is a humanized IgG1 monoclonal antibody that specifically binds to the p19 protein subunit of the IL-23 cytokine and inhibits its interaction with the IL-23 receptor. IL-23 is a naturally occurring cytokine, composed of 2 subunits (IL-23p19 and IL-12/23p40), that is involved in inflammatory and immune responses.
L49: The proposed indication is "the treatment of moderate to severe plaque psoriasis in adults." However, enrollment criteria for the clinical trials included "adults with moderate to severe plaque psoriasis who are candidates for systemic therapy or phototherapy". The risankizumab pharmaceutical formulation is a solution for injection at a concentration of 90 mg/mL presented in a pre-filled syringe (PFS) for subcutaneous (SC) injection. Each syringe contains 75 mg risankizumab. The proposed dosing regimen for patients with psoriasis is 150 mg (two 75 mg injections) administered by SC injection at Week 0, Week 4, and every 12 weeks (q12w) thereafter.
L50: The Agency concluded that the proposed proprietary name, Skyrizi, was acceptable from both a promotional and safety perspective [Proprietary Name Review by Carlos Mena-Grillasca, Division of Medication Error Prevention and Analysis (DMEPA) dated 7/6/2018].
L51: ## 1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L52: The applicant submitted data from two adequate and well-controlled [trials M15-995 (1311.28), M16-008 (1311.3)], which provided evidence of the effectiveness of risankizumab for the treatment of moderate to severe plaque psoriasis in adult patients who are candidates for systemic therapy or phototherapy. The trials assessed the changes from Baseline to Week 16 compared to placebo in the two co-primary endpoints:
L53: - the proportion of subjects who achieved at least a 90% reduction in the PASI composite score (PASI 90)
- the proportion of subjects who achieved a static Physician's Global Assessment (sPGA) score of 0 ("clear") or 1 ("almost clear").
L54: Risankizumab was statistically superior to placebo (p-values &lt; 0.001) on the co-primary endpoints. The applicant has demonstrated that risankizumab is effective for its intended use in the target population, and has met the evidentiary standard required by 21 CFR 314.126(a)(b) to support approval.
L55: ## 1.3. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L56: ## Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
L57: Psooriasis is a chronic, inflammatory disease that primarily affects the skin and is characterized by erythematous, scaly plaques and substantial impairment of quality of life
L58: Skyrizi (risankizumab) injection, for subcutaneous use is proposed for the treatment of adults with moderate to severe plaque psoriasis who are candidates for systemic therapy or phototherapy. Skyrizi (risankizumab) injection for subcutaneous use is a humanized IgG1 monoclonal antibody that specifically binds to the p19 protein subunit of the IL-23 cytokine and inhibits its interaction with the IL-23 receptor. IL-23 is a naturally occurring cytokine, composed of 2 subunits (IL-23p19 and IL-12/23p40), that is involved in inflammatory and immune responses. Risankizumab is available as 75 mg PFS to be administered as a 150 mg dose (two injections).
L59: For the treatment of moderate to severe plaque psoriasis, current therapeutic options include phototherapy and photochemotherapy with methoxsalen, systemic small molecule drugs (acitretin, apremilast, cyclosporine, methotrexate), and biologic products (adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab, ustekinumab, secukinumab, ixekizumab, brodalumab, guselkumab, and tildrakizumab). Although the efficacy varies, no product produces a response in all patients or provides a permanent cure. Phototherapy and photochemotherapy may be impractical due to office-based administration requirements. All the systemic products may have one or more serious adverse reactions, including malignancy, serious infections, teratogenicity, depression, nephrotoxicity, hepatotoxicity, and bone marrow suppression. Because of these limitations, there is a recognizable need for additional therapeutic options despite the number of available therapies.
L60: Substantial efficacy was demonstrated in two pivotal Phase 3 trials (M15-995 [1311.28], M16-008 [1311.3]). In these trials, 997 adult subjects with moderate to severe plaque psoriasis who were eligible for systemic therapy or phototherapy were treated with risankizumab 150 mg (N=598), placebo (N=200) or EU-sourced ustekinumab (N=199). Subjects had a Static Physician's Global Assessment (sPGA) score of \ge 3 ("moderate") on a 5-point scale of overall disease severity, a Psoriasis Area and Severity Index (PASI) score \ge 12, and a minimum affected body surface area (BSA) involvement of \ge 10\%. In both trials, subjects were randomized in a 3:1:1 ratio to risankizumab 150 mg, ustekinumab (EU-sourced) [45 mg for &lt;100\text{kg} and 90 mg for &gt;100\text{kg} weight] or placebo. Subjects received subcutaneous injections at Weeks 0 and 4, then every 12 (Q12) weeks.
L61: The co-primary endpoints were assessed at Week 16: i) the proportion of subjects who achieved an sPGA score of 0 ("clear") or 1 ("almost clear"), and ii) the proportion of subjects who achieved at least a 90% reduction in the PASI composite score (PASI 90). In Trials M15-995 [1311.28], M16-008 [1311.3], risankizumab was superior to placebo on both sPGA (Trial 1311.28: 84% vs 5% and Trial 1311.3: 88% vs 8%; p\text{-values} &lt; 0.001) and PASI 90 (Trial 1311.28: 75% vs 2% and Trial 1311.3: 75% vs 5%; p\text{-values} &lt; 0.001) at Week 12.
L62: Secondary endpoints for both trials included the proportion of subjects at Week 16 who achieved an sPGA of 0, PASI 100 and patient reported
L63: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – BLA 761105
## (Skyrizi, Risankizumab)
L64: outcomes [Psoriasis Symptom Scale (PSS) and Dermatology Life Quality Index (DLQI)]. In both trials, risankizumab was statistically superior to placebo (with p-values&lt;0.001) in the overall population for all the secondary efficacy endpoints. Evidence submitted by the applicant sufficiently demonstrates that the PSS is fit-for-purpose for labeling. It is recommended that the PSS is included in labeling. The DLQI does not appear to be fit-for-purpose for labeling in the context of this drug development program due to lack of item relevancy. Labeling for DLQI results are not recommended.
L65: In the clinical trials, the applicant used EU-approved ustekinumab and did not provide an adequate scientific bridge between the U.S. licensed Stelara and EU-sourced ustekinumab. (b)(4)
L66: To evaluate maintenance and durability of response, subjects in Trials 1311.28 and 1311.3 were followed to Week 52. For subjects receiving risankizumab in Trials 1311.28 and 1311.3 who were PASI 100 responders at Week 16, 80% of subjects who continued on risankizumab had a PASI 100 response at Week 52. For PASI 90 responders at Week 16, 88% of subjects had a PASI 90 response at Week 52. In Trial 1311.4, subjects were rerandomized at Week 28 to continue risankizumab every 12 weeks or to withdrawal therapy. For subjects who were clear or almost clear (sPGA 0/1) at Week 28, 87% re-randomized to continued treatment with risankizumab had an sPGA response of 0 or 1 at Week 52 compared to 61% who were re-randomized to withdrawal.
L67: The primary safety database, which consisted of data from the pooled Phase 2/3 Trials[1311.2, M15-995 (1311.28), M16-008 (1311.3), M16-010 (1311.30) and M15-992 (1311.4)] is adequate to characterize the safety profile of risankizumab 150 mg for subcutaneous injection in the treatment of psoriasis. The size of the overall safety database was considered adequate and consistent with ICH E1 and includes 2,234 subjects exposed to at least 1 dose of risankizumab and a total of 1,091 subjects receiving at least 1 dose of risankizumab 150 mg for ≥ 1 year.
L68: Treatment with risankizumab did not appear to increase the risk of mortality as compared to placebo through 16 weeks of randomized, blinded treatment. A limited number of serious adverse events (SAEs) in the risankizumab group led to discontinuation of study drug. An association between the product and malignancy was not observed. An increased incidence of treatment-related adverse reactions in the categories of suicidal ideation and behavior (SIB) and major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE) was not observed, given the limited clinical experience to date. Serious cases of hypersensitivity and injection site reactions did not occur. Some serious non-MACE cardiovascular and hepatic events occurred. Most of the events were confounded with concomitant medication or comorbid conditions. Compared to placebo, subjects treated with risankizumab experienced more infections, more serious infections and a higher rate of fungal infections. There were no reports of active TB. Subjects with active TB were excluded and those with latent TB were required to start treatment prophylaxis prior to starting on risankizumab. Language pertaining to infections and pre-treatment for tuberculosis is recommended for product labeling. The available data are insufficient to inform risankizumab-associated risk in pregnant women and children.
L69: Prescription and patient labeling, as well as pharmacovigilance are adequate to manage the risk of risankizumab in the postmarketing setting; a Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategy (REMS) is not needed. Postmarketing requirements under 505(o) will include: studies to evaluate maternal, fetal, and infant outcomes of women exposed to risankizumab during pregnancy, and a safety study to assess for malignancy and other serious adverse reactions (e.g., serious infections, tuberculosis, opportunistic infections, hypersensitivity reactions, autoimmune disease,
L70: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – BLA 761105
## (Skyrizi, Risankizumab)
L71: neurologic or demyelinating disease, cardiovascular, gastrointestinal and hematologic adverse events). Required pediatric assessments, under the Pediatric Research Equity Act (PREA) (21 U.S.C. 355c) will include: a safety, efficacy and PK study in pediatric subjects 6 years to &lt; 18 years of age with moderate to severe plaque psoriasis.
L72: The available safety and efficacy data support the approval of risankizumab 150 mg for subcutaneous injection for the treatment of adults with moderate to severe plaque psoriasis who are candidates for systemic therapy or phototherapy. Although there are a number of FDA-approved products with an acceptable benefit-risk profile for this indication, none of these treatments provides a permanent cure or universal response and all of these products are associated with one or more serious risks. Because treatment may be complicated by inadequate response, loss of response, adverse reactions, and the presence of comorbidities or concomitant illnesses, there is a need for additional therapeutic options.
L73: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of Condition	Psoriasis is a common, chronic, inflammatory multi-system disorder which primarily affects the skin and joints and is associated with substantial impairment of quality of life. The prevalence of psoriasis in the U.S. is approximately 2-3 %, of which an estimated 20% have moderate to severe disease. One third of patients have concomitant arthritis. Comorbidities that have been reported to be potentially associated with psoriasis include depression/suicide, autoimmune disease, cardiovascular disease, and metabolic syndrome.
Moderate to severe plaque psoriasis is a serious disease because of its chronicity, impact on quality of life, and potential for associated co-morbidities.
Current Treatment Options	FDA approved drugs for the treatment of moderate to severe psoriasis include anti-metabolites (methotrexate), tumor necrosis factor (TNF) inhibitors (etanercept, adalimumab and infliximab), IL-12/23 blockers (ustekinumab), IL-17A blockers (secukinumab and ixekizumab), an IL-17A receptor antagonist (brodalumab), IL-23 blockers (guselkumab and tildrakizumab), a T cell inhibitor (cyclosporine), retinoids (acitretin) and phosphodiesterase 4 inhibitors (apremilast). Other treatment options include phototherapy with either PUVA (UVA light combined with methoxsalen) or UVB light (narrow or broadband).
All approved therapeutic options may be associated with the risk of serious adverse reactions or administration challenges. The use of phototherapy and photochemotherapy are limited by the need for
There are several FDA-approved products with an acceptable benefit-risk profile for the treatment of moderate-to-severe plaque psoriasis in adults. None of these treatments provides a permanent cure or universal response and all these products are associated with one or more serious risks. Because treatment may be complicated by inadequate response, loss of response, adverse reactions, and the presence of comorbidities or concomitant illnesses, there is a need for additional therapeutic options.

L75: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
office administration and additional photoprotection. Teratogenicity and hyperlipidemia are labeled risks with acitretin. Depression and weight loss are safety concerns with apremilast. The primary risks of cyclosporine use are nephrotoxicity and hypertension. Methotrexate has teratogenic, hepatotoxic, and nephrotoxic effects and may cause bone marrow toxicity and pulmonary fibrosis. Other systemic products may cause immunosuppression, serious infections and malignancy. All biologic products may be associated with loss of effect and serious hypersensitivity reactions. See the Summary of Treatment Armamentarium for Moderate to Severe Psoriasis for the specific labeled safety issues for each product.

Benefit	Substantial efficacy was demonstrated in two pivotal Phase 3 trials (M15-995 [1311.28], M16-008 [1311.3]). In these trials, 997 adult subjects with moderate to severe plaque psoriasis who were eligible for systemic therapy or phototherapy were treated with risankizumab 150 mg (N=598), placebo (N=200) or EU-sourced ustekinumab (N=199). Subjects had a Static Physician's Global Assessment (sPGA) score of \ge 3 ("moderate") on a 5-point scale of overall disease severity, a Psoriasis Area and Severity Index (PASI) score \ge 12, and a minimum affected body surface area (BSA) involvement of \ge 10\%. In both trials, subjects were randomized in a 3:1:1 ratio to risankizumab 150 mg, ustekinumab (EU-sourced) [45 mg for <100kg and 90 mg for >100kg weight] or placebo. Subjects received subcutaneous injections at Weeks 0 and 4, then every 12 (Q12) weeks.
The co-primary endpoints were assessed at Week 16: i) the proportion of subjects who achieved an sPGA score of 0 ("clear") or 1 ("almost clear"), and ii) the proportion of subjects who achieved at least a 90% reduction in the PASI composite score (PASI 90). In Trials M15-995 [1311.28], M16-008 [1311.3], risankizumab was superior to placebo on both sPGA (Trial 1311.28: 84% vs 5% and Trial 1311.3: 88% vs 8%; p-values< 0.001) and PASI 90 (Trial
The data submitted by the applicant met the evidentiary standard for provision of substantial evidence of effectiveness under the proposed conditions of use. The trials were adequate and well-controlled. The results are persuasive.
Achievement of clear or almost-clear skin is an intrinsically meaningful outcome for a cutaneous disease such as psoriasis. The data suggest that a patient with moderate-to-severe plaque psoriasis treated with 150 mg risankizumab at Week 0, 4 and every 12 weeks is likely to achieve clear or almost clear skin by Week 16, and to maintain this effect with continued treatment to Week 52.

Risk Management	The following PMRs will be required:Conduct a prospective, registry based observational exposure cohort study that compares the maternal, fetal, and infant outcomes of women exposed to risankizumab during pregnancy to an unexposed control population. The registry will detect and record major and minor congenital malformations, spontaneous abortions, stillbirths, elective terminations, small for gestational age, and any other adverse pregnancy outcomes. These outcomes will be assessed throughout pregnancy. Infant outcomes, including neonatal deaths, infections in the first 6 months of life, and effects on postnatal growth and development, will be assessed through at least the first year of life.
Conduct (b) (4) retrospective cohort study using claims or electronic medical record data or a case control study) to assess major congenital malformations, spontaneous abortions, stillbirths, small for gestational age, neonatal deaths, and infant infections in women exposed to risankizumab during pregnancy compared to an unexposed control population.
Conduct an observational study to assess the long-term safety of risankizumab compared to other therapies used in the treatment of adults with moderate-to-severe plaque psoriasis who are candidates for systemic therapy or

L87: ## 2 Therapeutic Context
L88: ## 2.1. Analysis of Condition
L89: Psoriasis is a common, chronic, immune-mediated, inflammatory skin disorder with a strong hereditary component. The pathogenesis of psoriasis is complex and the etiology is not completely understood. Inflammatory cytokines appear to play a role in the pathogenesis and a number of cytokines have been targeted and have demonstrated therapeutic benefit.
L90: The prevalence of psoriasis in the US is approximately 2-4%, of which an estimated 20% have moderate to severe disease. Psoriasis can first appear at any age, but more commonly appears in adulthood. Two peaks in age of onset have been reported: one at 20-30 years of age and a second peak at 50-60 years.
L91: The most common form of the disease is chronic plaque psoriasis (*psoriasis vulgaris*). The characteristic lesion is a sharply demarcated scaly, erythematous plaque with scale, and the plaques may be localized or widespread in distribution and symmetrically distributed. These plaques are the result of increased keratinocyte proliferation and commonly involve the extensor surfaces of the extremities, but may present anywhere from the scalp to the soles of the feet. The natural history of psoriasis is chronic with intermittent remissions. A diagnosis of psoriasis can typically be made by history and physical examination, though skin biopsy can provide confirmation if needed.
L92: The extent of body surface area affected may vary from limited to extensive, and the extent of involvement is one consideration in the approach to patient management. Disease of limited extent may be effectively managed with topical treatment. Moderate to severe psoriasis is often treated with phototherapy or systemic therapy.
L93: Associations between psoriasis and other comorbid diseases are under investigation; it is postulated that psoriasis is part of a multisystem inflammatory disorder. The pathogenesis of comorbid disease in patients with psoriasis remains unknown.
L94: Due to the variability in effectiveness and tolerability, access to treatment and uncertainty regarding long-term effects of available treatments, the development of additional safe and effective therapies for the treatment of psoriasis continues to be an important goal.

L121: ## 3 Regulatory Background
L122: ## 3.1. U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
L123: Because risankizumab is an original BLA and is not currently marketed in the United States, this section is not applicable.
L124: ## 3.2. Summary of Presubmission/Submission Regulatory Activity
L125: The applicant developed risankizumab injection under IND 113306. On February 28, 2012, the applicant opened the IND. During their development program, the applicant interacted with the Agency at the following milestones/meetings: Pre-IND 01/18/2012; Guidance 04/05/2017, 03/29/2017, 11/16/2016 and 11/17/2015; Written Responses 06/14/2017, 11/04/2016 and 10/29/2015; End-of-Phase 2 (EOP2) 06/24/2015; Pre-BLA 12/12/2017.
L126: At the End-of-Phase 2 (EOP2) meeting the Agency made the following recommendations (how these were addressed by the applicant is in *italics*):
L127: - The 150 mg dose with dosing at Week 0, Week 4 and every 12 weeks thereafter for Phase 3 appears reasonable based on the provided information (*dose used in Phase 3 studies*)
- We recommend that you continue to evaluate the impact of body-weight on the efficacy and safety measures in the Phase 3 trials and submit these evaluations in the BLA submission to assist in reviewing the appropriateness of dosing for different body-weight ranges (*elected to examine a single dosage level and regimen in the phase 3 program, pre-specified subgroup analyses were performed to evaluate the impact of body-weight*)
- The proposed coprimary endpoint of PASI 90 and sPGA of clear/almost clear with 2- grade improvement with an adequate sPGA scale is acceptable. However, we cannot comment on acceptability of the proposed coprimary endpoint sPGA of clear/almost clear, as the briefing document did not contain a description (*scale was submitted with the protocol for review*)
- The design of the trials to evaluate safety is not adequate (*protocol was modified prior to submission*)
- We agree that a TQT study is not required. However, periodic EKG monitoring during the Phase 3 trials should be performed (*ECG monitoring was performed during the Phase 3 program*)
- One of your pivotal Phase 3 trials should include provisions for subjects to self-administer the drug product (*Study M16-010 [1311.30] was modified to require self-administration of study drug*)
- It may be reasonable to use EU-approved ustekinumab as an active control in a superiority clinical trial [REDACTED] (b) (4) if you can establish an adequate scientific bridge to justify the relevance of data obtained with EU-approved ustekinumab (*has not provided*)
L128: *bridging information, states that the performance of ustekinumab in terms of safety and effectiveness are expected to be the same whether a patient receives ustekinumab from the EU or US marketplace,* (b) (4)
L129: - The regulatory utility of the proposed trial is limited. Humira was approved after 16 weeks of therapy (i.e., an initial dose of 80 mg at Week 0 followed by a dose of 40 mg every other week starting Week 1 through Week 15) using the endpoints of PASI 75 and “clear” or “almost clear” on PGA. In your proposed trial, subjects should receive the approved dose regimen of Humira, and the comparison between your product and Humira should be done at Week 16 using the endpoints of PASI 75 and “clear” or “almost clear” on PGA. In addition, “incomplete response” should be defined as those who did not achieve “clear” or “almost clear” on PGA and PASI 75 at Week 16 (For Trial 1311.30, the sponsor revised their primary timepoint from Week 12 to Week 16, and proposed co-primary endpoints of sPGA 0 or 1 at Week 16 along with PASI 90 at Week 16 with PASI 75 at Week 16 as a secondary endpoint)
- The safety database that you propose of 1800 subjects exposed to BI 655066 at 150 mg dosing and of at least 1000 subjects being exposed at this dose for a year seems reasonable (achieved the proposed exposure)
L130: The applicant submitted three Phase 3 protocols (1311.3, 1311.28, and 1311.4) for review (not under a special protocol assessment) along with questions seeking specific advice on the protocols. The sPGA scale and safety monitoring was found to be acceptable. The intended study population (moderate to severe chronic plaque psoriasis BSA of \ge 10\%, PASI \ge 12, and IGA score of moderate) was found to be acceptable. Comments on the statistical hierarchy were provided. For Studies M15-995 (1311.28) and M16-008 (1311.3), the statistical testing hierarchy was re-ordered to prioritize comparisons with placebo.
L131: The applicant submitted three Phase 3 protocols new clinical protocol 1311.30 with modifications. The Agency provided the following comment (in advice letter March 9, 2016): To support a superiority claim against an active comparator, replication of study findings from two adequate and well-controlled trials is recommended. (b) (4)
L132: ## 4 Significant Issues from Other Review Disciplines Pertinent to Clinical Conclusions on Efficacy and Safety
L133: ## 4.1. Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI)
L134: The clinical sites chosen for inspection were primarily based on numbers of enrolled subjects, treatment effect, and prior inspectional history. The clinical sites of Drs. Toth, Hong, and Tyring and the study sponsors, AbbVie, Inc. and Boehringer Ingelheim Pharmaceuticals, Inc. (BI) were inspected in support of this BLA to verify data from study Protocols M15-992 (1311.4), M15-995 (1311.28), and M16-008 (1311.3). The European Medicines Agency (EMA) has received the same marketing authorization application (MAA), and FDA and EMA conducted joint inspections of the study sponsors, AbbVie, Inc. and BI, as part of the FDA-EMA GCP Initiative.
L135: During the sponsor inspection, the following data integrity concerns involving the Psoriasis Area and Severity Index (PASI) and Static Physician Global Assessment (sPGA) scores were identified:
L136: - Inappropriate use of a medical scribe that resulted in no clearly attributable signature of a qualified assessor that was necessary for verifying the accuracy of the assessment data entered by the medical scribe
- Incorrect data attribution
- Alteration of the audit trail information
L137: These observations identified above affected a small percentage of study subjects as follows:
L138: - 54/1504 (3.6%) subjects across all three trials for Visits 2 and 6 PASI scores
- 26/1504 (1.7%) subjects across all three trials for Visits 2 and 6 sPGA scores
L139: Refer to Section 7.2.1.8.2 for sensitivity analysis.
L140: Also noted were 718 cases of incorrect date and time stamps of PASI, sPGA, and Psoriasis Symptoms Scale (PSS) data that occurred when the batteries of the mobile devices used to capture this data were critically low. When date and time stamps could not be corrected, the Sponsor and the Contract Research Organization (CRO) who supplied the devices and data management services deleted the data. The date and time corrections did not impact the reliability of the data because we have confidence in the algorithm used to determine the actual dates and times the data were entered by the qualified assessors and study subjects.
L141: Notwithstanding these observations that affected the reliability of the primary efficacy endpoint data for a small percentage of the study population across all three protocols, the studies appear to have been conducted adequately. The study data, including the primary efficacy endpoint data for the rest of the study population, appear acceptable in support of the respective indication.
L142: The final compliance classification of the inspections of Drs. Toth and Hong was No Action Indicated (NAI). The final classification of the inspection of Dr. Tyring was Voluntary Action Indicated (VAI). The final classification of the inspection of the current sponsor, AbbVie, was NAI, and the final classification of the initial sponsor, BI, was VAI.

L162: **Description:** Risankizumab is a humanized monoclonal immunoglobulin G1 (IgG1 isotype) consisting of two identical heavy chains (HC) and two identical light chains (LC) covalently linked through inter- and intra-chain disulfide bonds. Each HC and LC are composed of 449 and 214 amino acids, respectively. Each HC contains a leucine to alanine substitution at amino acid residues 237 and 238 within the Fc domain to minimize the potential for effector function and deletion of lysine at the C terminus to reduce potential for charge heterogeneity. A single N-linked glycosylation site is in the CH2 domain of each HC at asparagine residue 297.
L163: The extinction coefficient was calculated and confirmed experimentally to be 1.52 \text{ mL} \times \text{mg}^{-1} \times \text{cm}^{-1} at 280 nm.
L164: **Mechanism of Action (MoA):** Risankizumab selectively binds to IL-23 p19 and inhibits binding of IL-23 to its receptor. This in turn, inhibits IL-23 inflammatory responses that contribute to the pathogenesis of plaque psoriasis. The generation and characterization of risankizumab was undertaken to include selectivity for the p19 over the p40 subunit, to overcome the high-affinity binding between IL-23 to IL-23R and favorable biophysical properties.
L165: **Potency Assay:** Biological activity of risankizumab is evaluated using a chicken lymphoma cell DT-40 line, which constitutively expresses the human IL-23R complex (IL-23R and IL-12R\beta1 subunits) and STAT5B. The DT-40 bioassay assay is a luciferase reporter-gene assay which measures the activation of the human IL-23R/STATB pathway. Upon IL-23 binding to the IL-23R, STAT5B is phosphorylated and activates transcription of STAT5B responsive elements and subsequently activates the firefly luciferase. The luciferase activity is then quantified. In the presence of risankizumab, IL-23 cannot bind the IL-23R, leading to the inhibition of luciferase activity. To measure risankizumab potency, DT-40 cells are co-incubated with increasing amounts of risankizumab and a 4-parameter (4-PL) unconstrained logistic analysis is used to calculate the dose response curve. Potency is reported as a percentage relative to the reference standard.

L203: ## **5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology**
L204: ## **5.1. Executive Summary**
L205: Risankizumab is a humanized recombinant IgG1 monoclonal antibody that selectively binds to the p19 subunit of human interleukin 23 (IL-23p19) and blocks the interaction of IL-23 with the IL-23 receptor. The Fc portion of risankizumab has two replacement mutations (Leu234Ala and Leu235Ala) to reduce Fc\gamma receptor and complement binding.
L206: Risankizumab displayed a high affinity binding to its target human IL-23 p19 in vitro, with no detectable binding to human IL-12 or rat IL-23 at the concentration of 1 \muM. Binding of risankizumab to Cynomolgus monkey IL-23 was also high, with a dissociation constant (Kd) of less than 1 pM. Risankizumab bound to mouse IL-23 with a Kd of 15 nM. Risankizumab inhibited IL-23-dependent phosphorylation of signal transducer and activator of transcription 3 (STAT3) in the human cell line DB with an IC50 value of 24 pM. Additionally, risankizumab inhibited the induction of IL-17 by
L207: human IL-23 in mouse splenocytes, but does not inhibit the induction of interferon gamma (IFN\gamma) by human IL-12 in phytohaemagglutinin (PHA) blasts. In an in vivo mouse ear thickening model, risankizumab inhibited human IL-23-induced ear thickening as well as the induction of IL-17 and IL-22 in the ear tissue. The Cynomolgus monkey was identified as the pharmacologically relevant species and used in toxicology studies.
L208: Intravenous treatment with doses up to 50 mg/kg/week risankizumab for 4 weeks did not cause treatment-related effects on neurological or cardiovascular functions in Cynomolgus monkeys. There were no treatment-related effects on respiratory rate or body temperature in Cynomolgus monkeys subcutaneously treated with doses up to 50 mg/kg/week risankizumab for 6 months.
L209: In a 6-month repeat-dose study with a 2-month recovery period, four groups of up to 6 male (4.1 to 6.4 years old) and up to 6 female (2.5 to 3.8 years old) Cynomolgus monkeys were treated subcutaneously with 0 (vehicle, [REDACTED](b) (4) succinic acid, [REDACTED](b) (4) disodium succinate hexahydrate, [REDACTED](b) (4) sorbitol, [REDACTED](b) (4) Polysorbate 20, pH [REDACTED](b) (4)), 5, 20, or 50 mg/kg risankizumab once weekly. There were no treatment-related effects on mortalities, clinical signs, neurobehavioral assessments, ophthalmology, body weights, body temperature, ECG, blood pressure, responses to KLH immunization (IgM and IgG antibody responses), clinical pathology including peripheral blood lymphocyte phenotyping, and macroscopic findings. Hematology analysis showed a slight dose-dependent change in reticulocytes that did not reverse. Findings of potentially treatment-related adverse changes in male reproductive organs/tissues were observed in risankizumab-treated males. However, upon further analysis it was determined that the male monkeys used in this study were not sexually mature. A follow-up study using sexually mature male monkeys was performed to evaluate possible treatment-related effects on male reproductive organs/tissues.
L210: Following subcutaneous administration of risankizumab at 50 mg/kg/week for 26 weeks in 10 sexually mature male Cynomolgus monkeys, there were no treatment-related effects on mortality, clinical signs, body weights, food consumption, hematology, clinical chemistry, testicular volume, male reproductive hormones (serum testosterone, LH, and FSH), semen analysis (sperm count, motility, and morphology; ejaculate volume), gross pathology, organ weights, and histopathological findings including progression of the spermatogenic cycle, cell associations, and cell proportions in the various stages of spermatogenesis. The C_{max} and AUC0-168h of risankizumab at the end of the dosing period were 950 \mug/mL and 122,000 \mug\cdoth/mL, respectively. The dose tested in this 26-week study, 50 mg/kg, is 20 times (based on mg/kg comparison) the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) in patients (approximately 2.5 mg/kg based on administration of a 150 mg dose to a 60 kg individual)
L211: In an enhanced pre- and post-natal toxicity study, three groups of 21 or 22 pregnant female Cynomolgus monkeys received subcutaneously 0 (vehicle, [REDACTED](b) (4) succinate, [REDACTED](b) (4) sorbitol, [REDACTED](b) (4) Polysorbate 20, pH [REDACTED](b) (4)), 5, or 50 mg/kg risankizumab once weekly from Gestation Day 20-22 (GD 20-22) until parturition for up
L212: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – BLA 761105
## (Skyrizi, Risankizumab)
L213: to 22 doses and post-natal development was evaluated in infants for 6 months after birth. There were no treatment-related effects on maternal mortality, clinical signs, body weights, food consumption, and gestation length. There were no treatment-related effects on fetal clinical signs, body weights, hematology, clinical chemistry, growth and development (including external, visceral, skeletal, and neurobehavioral evaluations), developmental immunotoxicology parameters (T-cell dependent antibody response and lymphocyte immunophenotyping), necropsy, organ weights, and histopathologic findings. However, as compared to the control group, a dose-dependent increase in fetal/infant loss was noted in the risankizumab-treated groups. The percent fetal/infant loss was 19%, 32%, and 43% in the vehicle control, 5 mg/kg, and 50 mg/kg groups, respectively. The increased fetal/infant loss in the 50 mg/kg/week group was considered to be related to risankizumab treatment (refer to Section 5.5.4.1 for detailed information concerning the criteria used for the determination of this treatment related effect). The AUC0-168h in pregnant monkeys treated at 5 or 50 mg/kg/week was 15,700 or 123,000 µg·h/mL on GD 132-134, respectively. All infants from mothers in the risankizumab-treated groups had measurable and dose-dependent serum concentrations of risankizumab up to 91 days postpartum, while none of the infants had measurable concentrations of risankizumab by 180 days postpartum. In the infants, mean serum concentrations were approximately 17-86% of the respective maternal concentrations. The NOAEL for maternal toxicity was identified as 50 mg/kg (20 times the MRHD, based on mg/kg comparison) and the NOAEL for developmental toxicity was identified as 5 mg/kg (2 times the MRHD, based on mg/kg comparison).
L214: No genetic toxicology or carcinogenicity studies were conducted with risankizumab. The applicant provided a carcinogenic risk assessment, mainly using information from published literature. Overall the risk for malignancy associated with long-term inhibition of IL-23 following administration of risankizumab to humans is considered to be low. No local irritation (erythema or edema) was noted at the injection site in rabbits injected intramuscularly or subcutaneously with risankizumab. Risankizumab did not cause effects on hemocompatibility with human blood in vitro.
L215: This BLA is approvable from a Pharmacology/Toxicology perspective. There is no recommended nonclinical PMC/PMR for this BLA.
L216: ## 5.2. Referenced NDAs, BLAs, DMFs
L217: All the pivotal nonclinical studies have been reviewed under INDs 113306 and (b) (4). Summary pharmacology/toxicology information is provided in this review. The code names used for Skyrizi (risankizumab) injection are BI 655066, 655066-01, ABBV-066, and PR-1691244.
L218: ## 5.3. Pharmacology
L219: ## Primary pharmacology
L220: ## 5.3.1. Binding assessment of BI 655066 to human and cyno IL-23
## (RD161521/BD0015)
L221: The binding affinity of BI 655066 to human IL-23 and cynomolgus monkey IL-23 was measured via ProteOn XPR36, a label free and micro-fluidics based technology. It showed that the binding affinity of BI 655066 to cynomolgus monkey IL-23 was high, with a dissociation constant (K_d) of less than 1 pM. However, the binding affinity of BI 655066 to human IL-23 varies from double digit pM to less than 1 pM.
L222: ## 5.3.2. Binding Assessment of BI 655066 to mouse and rat IL-23 and human IL-12
## (RD170961)
L223: The affinity of BI 655066 binding to mouse IL-23, rat IL-23, and human IL-12 was tested in vitro. BI 655066 at a concentration of 1 \muM did not bind to rat IL-23 or human IL-12. BI 655066 bound to mouse IL-23 with a K_d of 15 nM.
L224: ## 5.3.5. Inhibition of IL-17 production in IL-23-stimulated mouse splenocytes by BI 655066 (RD161518/BD0011)
L225: The ability of BI 655066 to inhibit the production of mouse IL-17 stimulated by IL-23 was evaluated in vitro. No inhibition of IL-17 by BI 655066 (up to 33 nM) was observed when the cells were treated with rat or mouse IL-23. BI 655066 did inhibit IL-17 production in freshly prepared mouse splenocytes stimulated by human and cynomolgus monkey IL-23. The average IC_{50} was 6.7 \pm 1.0 pM (mean \pm SEM) against recombinant human IL-23 with a linker, 7.3 \pm 2.8 pM against recombinant human IL-23 without a linker, 2.1 \pm 0.8 pM against human endogenous IL-23, and 16.3 \pm 4.5 pM against recombinant cynomolgus IL-23.
L226: ## 5.3.6. Inhibition of IL-23 induced STAT3 phosphorylation in the DB cell line by BI 655066 (RD161519/BD-0013)
L227: The ability of BI 655066 to inhibit IL-23-induced STAT3 phosphorylation was evaluated in a human B cell line (DB cells). BI 655066 was found to inhibit phosphorylation of STAT3 induced by human IL-23 in DB cells, with IC_{50} values ranging between 13 - 36 pM.
L228: ## 5.3.7. Demonstration of lack of cross-reactivity of BI 655066 with human IL-12
## (RD161522/BD0016)
L229: Human peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs) from 10 different donors were stimulated with Lectin from *Phaseolus vulgaris* (PHA) for 3 days. After incubation of these PBMCs with recombinant human IL-12 and test antibodies at 37 °C for 48 hr, IFN-\gamma in culture supernatants was quantitated. The control antibodies against IL-12 p40 and p70 demonstrated an ability to inhibit IFN-\gamma production induced by 0.1 or 1 ng/mL IL-12. However, no inhibition of IFN-\gamma production induced by 0.1 or 1 ng/mL IL-12 was seen when PHA-stimulated human PBMCs were treated with of 5 \mug/mL (33 nM) or 10
L230: \mug/mL (66 nM) BI 655066, indicating a lack of functional cross reactivity of BI 655066 with recombinant human IL-12.
L231: ## 5.3.8. In vivo activity of anti-human p19 antibody BI 655066 in IL-23 induced skin inflammation model (RD161523/IM2580)
L232: Recombinant human IL-23 injected daily for 4 consecutive days resulted in a significant increase in ear thickness which is accompanied by increases in IL-17 and IL-22 in the ear tissue of mice. Treating mice prior to the initial IL-23 challenge with a single dose of BI 655066 at 1 mg/kg resulted in significantly reduced ear thickness as well as both IL-17 and IL-22 levels in the ear tissue, compared to the vehicle-treated controls challenged by IL-23. In addition, BI 655066 appeared to be more potent than Stelara (a clinically available antibody targeting the p40 subunit of both IL-23 and IL-12) at inhibiting IL-23 induced cytokine production in this mouse model.
L233: ## Safety Pharmacology
L234: Intravenous treatment with doses up to 50 mg/kg/week risankizumab for 4 weeks did not cause treatment-related effects on neurological or cardiovascular functions in Cynomolgus monkeys. There were no treatment-related effects on respiratory rate or body temperature in Cynomolgus monkeys subcutaneously treated with doses up to 50 mg/kg/week risankizumab for 6 months.
L235: ## 5.4. ADME/PK
L236: ## 5.4.1. Pharmacokinetics and immunogenicity of BI 655066 in Cynomolgus monkeys receiving a single intravenous or subcutaneous dose of BI 655066 (RD161476)
L237: Two groups of 3 female monkeys received a single intravenous dose of 1.0 mg/kg or subcutaneous dose of 1.5 mg/kg risankizumab. Blood samples for pharmacokinetic analysis were collected at pre-dosing and at 0.167 (IV only), 0.5, 2, 8, 24, 48, 72, 96, 120, 168, 240, 336, 504, 672, 840, and 1008 hr post-dosing. Following intravenous treatment, the serum concentration-time profiles of risankizumab was characterized by a short distribution phase of approximately 1 - 2 days followed by a longer terminal phase. The terminal half-life (t_{1/2}) averaged 173 hr. Clearance (CL) of risankizumab averaged 0.243 mL/h/kg and steady-state volume of distribution (V_{ss}) averaged 40.7 mL/kg. Following subcutaneous treatment, maximum serum concentrations of risankizumab occurred at 48 - 72 hr and the terminal half-life averaged 184 hr. The bioavailability of BI 655066 following subcutaneous dosing was 71.8%.
L238: ## 5.4.2. BI 655066: A 26-Week subcutaneous toxicity and toxicokinetics study in Cynomolgus monkeys with an 8-week recovery period (RD161444/12R003)
L239: No risankizumab was detected in samples from the control group animals. The systemic exposure of risankizumab (C_{max} and AUC_{0-168h}) increased in an approximately dose-proportional manner on Day 1 and in Week 26 and no more than 2.2-fold upon repeated dosing at 50 mg/kg/week for 26 weeks. The AUC_{0-168h} of risankizumab in the 5, 20, and 50 mg/kg/week groups were 5,695, 21,350, and 55,150 \mu g \cdot h/mL on Day 1 and 12,250, 37,350, and 86,250 \mu g \cdot h/mL in Week 26, respectively.
L240: ## 5.4.3. A 26-week reproductive toxicity study with BI 655066 in male Cynomolgus Monkeys, with an 8-Week Recovery Period (RD161448)
L241: Plasma levels of risankizumab were measured in the samples collected pre-dosing and at 24, 48, 72, 96, and 168 hr post-dosing on Days 1 and 176, and pre-dosing on Days 29 and 92. Plasma concentrations of risankizumab were below LLOQ (30 ng/mL) in all samples from the control group and pre-dosing samples on Day 1. The systemic exposure of risankizumab (C_{max} and AUC_{0-168h}) increased less than threefold upon repeated dosing at 50 mg/kg/week for 26 weeks. The C_{max} and AUC_{0-168h} were 380 \mu g/mL and 50,300 \mu g \cdot h/mL on Day 1 and 950 \mu g/mL and 122,000 \mu g \cdot h/mL on Day 176, respectively.
L242: ## 5.4.4. Enhanced pre-postnatal toxicity of BI 655066 administered by subcutaneous injection in pregnant Cynomolgus monkeys with 6-month postnatal evaluation (RD170191)
L243: Blood samples were collected from pregnant monkeys pre-dosing and at 24, 48, 72, 96, and 168 hr post-dosing on GD 20 and GD 132, pre-dosing on GDs 34, 48, 76, 104, and 146, and PPD 14, 28, 91, and 180. Plasma concentrations of risankizumab were below LLOQ (30 ng/mL) in all samples from the control group. The systemic exposure of risankizumab (C_{max} and AUC_{0-168h}) increased in a dose-proportional manner. A moderate (approximately 2- to 3-fold) accumulation of risankizumab (C_{max} and AUC_{0-168h}) was observed upon repeated dosing. The AUC_{0-168h} in pregnant monkeys treated at 5 or 50 mg/kg/week was 4,690 or 49,400 \mu g \cdot h/mL on GD 20-22 and 15,700 or 123,000 \mu g \cdot h/mL on GD 132-134, respectively. Following delivery, most adult female monkeys in the risankizumab-treated groups had measurable serum concentrations of risankizumab up to 91 days postpartum, but (with the exception of one adult female at 50 mg/kg/week) serum concentrations in adult females were below detectable levels at 180 days postpartum.
L244: Plasma levels of risankizumab were also measured in the samples collected from infants on BDs 14, 28, 91, and 180. All infants from mothers in the risankizumab-treated groups had measurable and dose-dependent serum concentrations of risankizumab up to 91 days after birth, while none of the infants had measurable concentrations of risankizumab by 180 days after birth. The serum concentrations of risankizumab in infants and mothers decreased postpartum without risankizumab treatment. The mean infant serum exposure in the 5 mg/kg/week group was 25%, 31%, and 68% of the mean maternal serum exposure on BD 14, BD 28, and BD 91, respectively. The mean infant serum exposure in the 50 mg/kg/week group was 17%,
L245: 36%, and 86% of the mean maternal serum exposure on BD 14, BD 28, and BD 91, respectively.
L246: ## 5.5. Toxicology
L247: ## 5.5.1. General Toxicology
L248: ## 5.5.1.1. BI 655066: A 26-Week subcutaneous toxicity and toxicokinetics study in Cynomolgus monkeys with an 8-week recovery period (RD161444/12R003)
L249: In this 6-month repeat-dose study with a 2-month recovery period, four groups of up to 6 male (4.1 to 6.4 years old) and up to 6 female (2.5 to 3.8 years old) Cynomolgus monkeys were dosed subcutaneously with 0 (vehicle, [REDACTED](b) (4) succinate, [REDACTED](b) (4) sorbitol, [REDACTED](b) (4) Polysorbate 20, pH [REDACTED](b) (4)), 5, 20, or 50 mg/kg risankizumab (BI 655066) once weekly. There were no treatment-related effects on mortalities, clinical signs, neurobehavioral assessments, ophthalmology, body weights, body temperature, ECG, blood pressure, responses to KLH immunization (IgM and IgG antibody responses), clinical pathology including peripheral blood lymphocyte phenotyping, and macroscopic findings. Hematology analysis showed a slight dose-dependent change in reticulocytes that did not reverse. Males developed a decrease in the weight of the prostate (LD -24%, MD -18%, HD -25%, normalized to brain), which did not reverse after the recovery period (HD -37%, normalized to brain). There was also a decrease in the weight of the epididymis (-27%) and testes (-35%) weight at the HD, with reversal after the recovery period. The change in testicular weight was associated with bilateral, multifocal hypospermatogenesis, which developed in a dose dependent manner [one low-dose male (Grade 1), one mid-dose male (Grade 2) and two high-dose males (Grade 2)], based on both incidence and severity. The testicular finding was reversible. The NOAEL of risankizumab was determined to be 5 mg/kg in males (AUC 12,300 µg·h/mL) and 50 mg/kg in females (AUC 77,200 µg·h/mL).
L250: A Pathology Working Group (PWG) composed of veterinary pathologists experienced in the evaluation of the male cynomolgus monkey reproductive system was convened to review the testicular findings from this 26-week toxicology study. The PWG considered the hypospermatogenesis unrelated to treatment with risankizumab, but rather reflected normal testicular development in young Cynomolgus monkeys. Because of the testicular findings in this 26-week toxicity study in monkeys, a follow-up study using sexually mature male monkeys was performed.
L251: ## 5.5.1.2. A 26-week reproductive toxicity study with BI 655066 in male Cynomolgus Monkeys, with an 8-Week Recovery Period (RD161448/14R097)
L252: Two groups of 10 sexually mature male Cynomolgus monkeys (5.3 to 6.9 years of age with 5.3 to 7.6 kg body weight) were treated with vehicle [REDACTED](b) (4) succinic acid, [REDACTED](b) (4) disodium succinate hexahydrate, [REDACTED](b) (4) sorbitol, [REDACTED](b) (4) Polysorbate 20, pH [REDACTED](b) (4)) or 50 mg/kg risankizumab (BI 655066) subcutaneously once
L253: weekly for 26 consecutive weeks (26 doses total). At the end of the dosing phase (Day 184), 7 males/group were euthanized and necropsied; 3 males/group were euthanized and necropsied after an 8-week recovery period (Day 239). Following subcutaneous administration of risankizumab at 50 mg/kg/week for 26 weeks, there were no treatment-related effects on mortality, clinical signs, body weights, food consumption, hematology, clinical chemistry, testicular volume, male reproductive hormones (serum testosterone, LH, and FSH), semen analysis (sperm count, motility, and morphology; ejaculate volume), gross pathology, organ weights, and histopathological findings including progression of the spermatogenic cycle, cell associations, and cell proportions in the various stages of spermatogenesis. The C_{max} and AUC_{0-168h} at the end of the dosing period were 950 \mug/mL and 122,000 \mug\cdoth/mL, respectively.
L254: ## **5.5.1.3. BI 655066: A 4-week intravenous and subcutaneous toxicity and toxicokinetic study in Cynomolgus monkeys with an 8-week recovery period**
## (RD161429/11R060)
L255: Five groups of up to 5 male and up to 5 female Cynomolgus monkeys received intravenous doses of 0 [vehicle, [REDACTED] (b) (4) Succinate (pH (b) (4)), [REDACTED] (b) (4), and [REDACTED] (b) (4)], 5, 20, or 50 mg/kg risankizumab (BI 655066) OR subcutaneous doses of 20 mg/kg risankizumab once weekly for 4 weeks. There were no treatment-related effects on mortality, clinical signs, body weights, ophthalmoscopy, ECG, neurological examinations, clinical pathology, gross pathology, organ weights, and histopathological examinations. The AUC_{0-168h} for the males and females that received 50 mg/kg/week intravenously was 85,100 and 98,900 \mug\cdoth/mL, respectively. The AUC_{0-168h} for the males and females that received 20 mg/kg/week subcutaneously was 25,800 and 27,500 \mug\cdoth/mL, respectively.
L256: ## **5.5.2. Genetic Toxicology**
L257: Genetic toxicology studies were not needed to support the development and approval of risankizumab, per the ICH S6(R1) guidance.
L258: ## **5.5.3. Carcinogenicity**
L259: Conventional rodent carcinogenicity studies were not conducted for the evaluation of the carcinogenic potential of risankizumab, as rodents are not a pharmacologically relevant species. A carcinogenicity study with long-term antagonism of IL23p19 in rodents will not be informative because rodents are not a pharmacologically relevant species. Concurrence has been received from the Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee of CDER that a 2-year rodent carcinogenicity study is not needed. The applicant provided a weight-of-evidence analysis of available information to address the carcinogenic potential of risankizumab. In general, immunosuppressive drugs tend to be associated with increased cancer risk. However, no tumors or histopathological evidence of pre-neoplastic changes were
L260: observed in organs or tissues examined following subcutaneous administration of risankizumab to monkeys at dose levels up to 50 mg/kg once weekly for 26 weeks followed by a 8-week post-dosing observation period. The immune function tests in the monkey toxicity studies showed that risankizumab did not cause immunosuppression [(as assessed by T-dependent antibody response (TDAR)] or deplete T-cells. Animal data from available literature suggests that a risk for malignancy may be reduced in the case of IL-23 inhibition but also reduced in the case of exogenous administration of IL-23. Overall the risk for malignancy associated with long-term inhibition of IL-23 following administration of risankizumab to humans is considered to be low. Therefore, no additional nonclinical studies are recommended to evaluate the carcinogenic potential of risankizumab. Adequate labeling based on animal data from the sponsor and from literature as well as post-marketing patient monitoring of malignancy will be sufficient at this time.
L261: ## 5.5.4. Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
L262: ## 5.5.4.1. Enhanced pre-postnatal toxicity of BI 655066 administered by subcutaneous injection in pregnant Cynomolgus monkeys with 6-month postnatal evaluation (RD170191)
L263: Three groups of 21 or 22 pregnant female Cynomolgus monkeys subcutaneously received 0 (vehicle, (b) (4) succinate, (b) (4) sorbitol, (b) (4) Polysorbate 20, pH (b) (4) 5, or 50 mg/kg risankizumab (BI 655066) once weekly from Gestation Day 20-22 (GD 20-22) until parturition for up to 22 doses and post-natal development was evaluated in infants for 6 months after birth. The infants were euthanized on Birth Day (BD) 180±2. There were no treatment-related effects on maternal mortality, clinical signs, body weights, food consumption, and gestation length. There were no treatment-related effects on fetal clinical signs, body weights, hematology, clinical chemistry, growth and development (including external, visceral, skeletal, and neurobehavioral evaluations), developmental immunotoxicology (T-cell dependent antibody response and lymphocyte immunophenotyping), necropsy, organ weights, and histopathologic findings. However, as compared to the vehicle control group, a dose-dependent increase in fetal/infant losses were noted in the risankizumab-treated groups (see the following table). The fetal/infant loss (percentage) in the control group in this study was below the Testing Facility historical range (20.0%-42.9%). The fetal/infant loss in the 5 mg/kg/week group (31.8%) was comparable to the historical average (28.8%) of the Testing Facility. Although the combined fetal/infant loss (percentage) in the 50 mg/kg/week group (42.9%) was at the high end of the performing laboratory's background incidence rate for monkeys, it was over two times the increase compared to the control group (19.0%). Therefore, the increased fetal/infant loss in the 50 mg/kg/week group was considered to be related to risankizumab treatment. The AUC0-168h in pregnant monkeys treated at 5 or 50 mg/kg/week was 15,700 or 123,000 µg·h/mL on GD 132-134, respectively. All infants from mothers in the risankizumab-treated groups had measurable and dose-dependent serum concentrations of risankizumab up to 91 days after birth, while none
L264: of the infants had measurable concentrations of risankizumab by 180 days after birth (see Section 5.4.4. above)

L277: A local tolerance study was conducted in rabbits. Two groups of 4 male rabbits received single intramuscular and subcutaneous injections of 1 mL of vehicle (b) (4) disodium succinate hexahydrate, (b) (4) succinic acid, (b) (4) sorbitol, (b) (4) polysorbate 20, pH (b) (4) or 90 mg/mL risankizumab (BI 655066) in the vehicle. There were no treatment-related effects on clinical signs, body weights, local irritation (erythema, edema), gross necropsy, and histopathologic findings on injection sites.
L278: The cross-reactivity of risankizumab (BI 655066) was evaluated with cryosections of a panel of normal human tissues. Risankizumab staining was observed

## (Skyrizi, Risankizumab)
L280: only on extracellular granular material in the placenta, which was expected as there is IL-23 expression in several placental tissue elements and IL-23 p19 is a secreted protein. Additionally, no effects of risankizumab on hemocompatibility were noted with human blood in vitro.
L281: ## 6 Clinical Pharmacology
L282: ## 6.1. Executive Summary
L283: Risankizumab (also known as ABBV-066 and BI655066) is a humanized immunoglobulin G1 (IgG1) monoclonal antibody (mAb) that selectively binds to the p19 subunit of human interleukin (IL)-23.
L284: - **Proposed indication:** For the treatment of moderate to severe plaque psoriasis in adults.
- **Proposed dosing regimen:** 150 mg (two 75 mg injections) administered by subcutaneous (SC) injection at Weeks 0, 4, and every 12 weeks (Q12W) thereafter.
- **Proposed dosage form and presentation:** A single-dose pre-filled syringe (PFS) with 75 mg of risankizumab in 0.83 mL of solution (90 mg/mL).
L285: The Applicant evaluated the 150 mg risankizumab dosing regimen in four Phase 3 trials. Risankizumab was administered by SC injection at Weeks 0, 4, and Q12W thereafter. The Applicant conducted a Phase 2 dose-ranging trial to support the dose selection for Phase 3 trials. The Applicant also submitted the results of 3 Phase 1 trials in healthy subjects and subjects with psoriasis to support clinical pharmacology information of risankizumab.
L286: The key review findings with specific recommendations and comments are summarized in **Table 3**.
L287: **Table 3** Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Review
L288: Review Issues	Recommendations and Comments
Pivotal or supportive evidence of effectiveness	The efficacy of risankizumab for the treatment of moderate to severe psoriasis is established in four Phase 3 trials (M16-008 [1311.3], M15-992 [1311.4], M15-995 [1311.28], and M16-010 [1311.30]).
Dose-response and exposure-response (E-R) analysis for efficacy based on data from the Phase 2 dose-ranging trial (1311.2) and the four Phase 3 trials provide supportive evidence for effectiveness.

General dosing instructions	The efficacy data from Phase 3 trials overall support that the proposed dosing regimen (150 mg risankizumab administered by SC injection at Weeks 0, 4, and Q12W thereafter) is acceptable.

Dosing in patient subgroups (intrinsic and extrinsic factors)	No dose individualization is recommended based on intrinsic or extrinsic factors
L289: Drug interactions	Risankizumab has no clinically meaningful effect on the PK of drugs metabolized by cytochrome P450 (CYP)1A2, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, or CYP3A.

Immunogenicity	Subjects who developed anti-drug antibodies (ADA) with titer values \ge 128 were associated with reduced risankizumab concentrations and reduced efficacy.
Subjects who developed ADA appeared to be more likely to experience injection-site reaction compared to subjects who were ADA negative.

Bridge between the to-be-marketed and clinical trial formulations	The to-be-marketed formulation was used in Phase 3 trials; therefore, there is no need to bridge the to-be-marketed formulation to the clinical trial formulation
L290: ## 6.1.1. Recommendation
L291: From a Clinical Pharmacology standpoint, this BLA is acceptable to support the approval of SKYRIZI (risankizumab) for the treatment of moderate to severe plaque psoriasis in adults, provided that the Applicant and the Agency come to a mutually satisfactory agreement regarding the labeling.
L292: ## 6.1.2. Post-Marketing Requirements and Commitments (PMR/PMC)
L293: None
L294: ## 6.2. Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment
L295: ## 6.2.1. Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
L296: ## 6.2.1.1 Mechanism of action and pharmacodynamics
L297: Risankizumab is a humanized IgG1 mAb that selectively binds to the p19 subunit of human IL-23. Risankizumab inhibits IL-23 dependent cell signaling and release of pro-inflammatory cytokines. The pharmacodynamic (PD) effect of risankizumab in humans has not been characterized.
L298: ## 6.2.1.2 Pharmacokinetics of risankizumab
L299: Risankizumab exhibited linear PK with dose-proportional increase in exposure following a SC injection of doses ranging from 18 mg to 300 mg. The peak plasma concentration (C_{max}) was reached by 3-14 days. The absolute bioavailability of risankizumab was estimated to be 89% following SC injection.
L300: Based on population PK analysis, risankizumab CL, volume of distribution at steady state and t_{1/2} were estimated to be 0.31 L/day, 11.2 L, and 28 days, respectively, for a typical 90 kg subject. The inter-individual variability (%CV) for risankizumab clearance and volume of distribution were 24% and 34%, respectively. At the recommended dosing regimen, 150 mg SC at Weeks 0, 4, and Q12W thereafter, steady-state plasma concentrations were achieved by Week 16. The estimated steady-state C_{max} and trough concentrations (C_{trough}) were approximately 12 mcg/mL and 2 mcg/mL, respectively.
L301: ## 6.2.1.3 Drug interactions
L302: The pharmacokinetic (PK) results of the clinical drug interaction study (M16-007[1311.36]) suggest that risankizumab has no clinically meaningful effect on the PK of drugs metabolized by cytochrome P450 (CYP)1A2, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, or CYP3A. See section 6.3.2.4.
L303: ## 6.2.1.4 Immunogenicity
L304: Among 1288 evaluable subjects in the Phase 3 studies, approximately 19% of subjects treated with risankizumab developed ADA by Week 16. Of the subjects who developed ADA, approximately 42% (8% of all subjects) had neutralizing antibodies (NAb). The ADA and NAb incidences were 24% and 14% (57% of the subjects who developed ADA), respectively, among 1079 evaluable subjects by Week 52. Higher ADA titer appears to have an impact on the PK of risankizumab. A total of 16 subjects (1.5% of ADA evaluable subjects) had ADA titer values \ge 128 and these subjects were associated with reduced risankizumab concentrations and reduced efficacy.
L305: ## 6.2.2. General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
L306: ## 6.2.2.1 General Dosing
L307: The dose-response results in the Phase 2 study, the efficacy results in Phase 3 studies, and the integrated exposure-response (E-R) analyses results overall support the acceptability of the proposed dosing regimen, 150 mg at Weeks 0, 4, and Q12W thereafter.
L308: ## 6.2.2.2 Therapeutic Individualization
L309: Therapeutic individualization based on intrinsic or extrinsic factors is not necessary. Population PK analysis identified body weight (BW) as a significant covariate on risankizumab PK. Risankizumab concentrations were lower in subjects with higher BW. However, dose adjustment based on BW is not necessary based on the available efficacy data from the Phase 3 trials. See Section 6.3.2.3.
L310: ## 6.2.3 Outstanding Issues
L311: There are no outstanding issues that would preclude the approval of risankizumab from a Clinical Pharmacology's perspective.
L312: ## 6.3. Comprehensive Clinical Pharmacology Review
L313: ## 6.3.1. General Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetic Characteristics
L314: General clinical pharmacology, pharmacokinetics and immunogenicity of risankizumab are summarized in Table 4.
L315: **Table 4 Summary of Clinical Pharmacology and Immunogenicity of Risankizumab**
L316: Pharmacology
Mechanism of Action	Risankizumab is a humanized IgG1 mAb that selectively binds to the p19 subunit of human IL-23 and inhibits its interaction with IL-23 receptor. IL-23 is a naturally occurring cytokine that is involved inflammatory and immune responses. Risankizumab inhibits IL-23 dependent cell signaling and the release of proinflammatory cytokines.
Pharmacodynamics	The pharmacodynamic (PD) effect of risankizumab in subjects with psoriasis has not been characterized.
General Information
Bioanalysis	Risankizumab concentrations in human plasma were quantified using a validated enzyme-linked immunosorbent (ELISA) assay with the sensitivity of 5 ng/mL.
PK model	A two-compartment model with a first-order absorption for SC administration and first-order elimination adequately described the PK of risankizumab
Healthy vs. Patients	Risankizumab PK parameters were similar in healthy subjects and subjects with moderate to severe psoriasis.
Drug Exposure at Steady State	Steady-state plasma concentrations of risankizumab were achieved approximately by Week 16 following SC administration of 150 mg risankizumab at Weeks 0, 4, and Q12W thereafter. Geometric mean trough concentrations (C_{trough}) in phase 3 trials at Weeks 16, 28, 40, and 52 were 1.84 mcg/mL, 1.60 mcg/mL, 1.57 mcg/mL, and 1.52 mcg/mL, respectively. The inter-individual variability (%CV) for C_{trough} ranged from 60% to 75%.
Dose Proportionality	Risankizumab showed dose proportional PK at the dose ranges of 18 – 300 mg and 200 - 1200 mg following single SC and IV administration, respectively.
Body weight	Risankizumab concentrations were lower in subjects with higher body weight. Population PK analysis also identified body weight as a significant covariate affecting risankizumab concentrations.
L317: ADME
Absorption	Following a single SC dose of 1 mg/kg to subjects with psoriasis, risankizumab reached geometric mean (%CV) C_{max} of 5.7 mcg/mL (19%) by approximately 5 days. Following SC administration of 150 mg risankizumab at Weeks 0, 4, and Q12W thereafter, the C_{max} was estimated to be achieved after the second risankizumab dose (Week 4) with mean \pm SD of 15.4 \pm 4.0 mcg/mL. The steady-state risankizumab C_{max} was estimated to be 11.9 \pm 3.1 mcg/mL. The absolute SC bioavailability of risankizumab was estimated to be 89%.
Distribution	The geometric mean (%CV) volume of distribution at steady state was 11.2 L (34%) in subjects with psoriasis.
Elimination	The geometric mean (%CV) systemic clearance was 0.31 L/day (24%) and half-life was approximately 28 days in subjects with psoriasis.
Metabolism	The metabolic pathway of risankizumab has not been characterized. As a humanized IgG1 mAb, risankizumab is expected to be degraded into small peptides and amino acids via catabolic pathways in a manner similar to endogenous IgG.
Excretion	The renal excretion of risankizumab has not been investigated. As a humanized IgG1 mAb with a molecular weight of approximately 148 kDa, intact risankizumab is unlikely to be filtered by kidney or excreted in urine.
Immunogenicity
Bioanalysis	Anti-drug antibodies (ADA) were determined using a validated electrochemiluminescent (ECL) assay with an acid dissociation step.
Neutralizing antibodies (NAb) were determined using a validated cell-based assay.

Incidence	Among 1288 evaluable subjects in the Phase 3 studies, approximately 19% of subjects treated with risankizumab developed ADA by Week 16. Of the subjects who developed ADA, approximately 42% (8% of all subjects) had neutralizing antibodies (NAb). The ADA and NAb incidences were 24% and 14% (57% of the subjects who developed ADA), respectively, among 1079 evaluable subjects by Week 52.
L318: Clinical Impact	PK: Antibodies to risankizumab did not appear to affect risankizumab concentrations when ADA titer values were <128. The mean risankizumab concentrations in subjects who had ADA titer values ≥128 were approximately 30% lower compared with the concentrations in ADA negative subjects.
Efficacy: The development of ADA with antibody titer values ≥128 was associated with reduced efficacy.
Safety: Subjects who developed antibodies to risankizumab were more likely to experience injection-site reactions compared to subjects who were ADA negative.
L319: ADME=Absorption, Distribution, Metabolism and Excretion.
L320: ## 6.3.2. Clinical Pharmacology Questions
L321: ## 6.3.2.1 *Does the clinical pharmacology information provide supportive evidence of effectiveness?*
L322: Yes. The overall data from Phase 2 and Phase 3 trials provide evidence that risankizumab is effective for the treatment of adult patients with moderate to severe psoriasis. The dose- and exposure-response relationships for efficacy provide supportive evidence of effectiveness. See Section 7 of this multi-discipline review for details of the study design and efficacy/safety results of the Phase 3 trials.
L323: ## *Dose-response relationship for efficacy in Phase 2 trial*
L324: The Phase 2 efficacy results demonstrated a clear dose-response relationship for PASI 90 and sPGA 0/1 (Figure 1). The PASI 90 response rates were 32.6%, 73.2% and 81.0% at the 18 mg, 90 mg, and 180 mg risankizumab dose groups, respectively. The sPGA 0/1 response rates were 62.8%, 90.2% and 90.5% at the 18 mg, 90 mg, and 180 mg risankizumab dose groups, respectively. Note that PASI 90 was the primary efficacy endpoint for the evaluation of dose-response relationship for efficacy in the Phase 2 trial.

L328: ## *Exposure-response relationship for efficacy in pooled Phase 2/3 trials*
L329: The Applicant used an E_{max} non-linear regression model with average plasma concentrations of risankizumab (C_{avg}) from Week 0 to Week 16 to describe exposure-response relationships for PASI75, PASI90, PASI100 and sPGA0/1 at Week 16 based on data from pooled Phase 2 and Phase 3 trials. The results demonstrated exposure-response relationships for the four efficacy variables.

L333: ## 6.3.2.2 *Is the proposed dosing regimen appropriate for the general patient population for which the indication is being sought?*
L334: Yes. The efficacy and safety data from Phase 3 trials overall support evidence that the proposed risankizumab 150 mg dosing regimen, administered by SC injection at Weeks 0, 4, and Q12W thereafter, is appropriate for the general patient population.
L335: The risankizumab 150 mg dosing regimen demonstrated consistent clinical response rates ranging from 72% to 75% for PASI 90 and from 84% to 88% for sPGA 0/1 at Week 16 across the four Phase 3 trials. See section 7 of this multidisciplinary review for details of the efficacy data. Exposure-response analysis indicates that the proposed 150 mg dosage regimen has achieved the plateau of efficacy and that higher doses would not result in a meaningful increase in clinical response rate (See section 6.3.2.1).
L336: No apparent exposure-response relationship for safety was observed by Week 16 in Phase 3 trials of risankizumab in subjects with moderate to severe psoriasis. The incidences of any adverse event (AE), any serious adverse event (SAE), infections and infestation, and any serious infection were similar across the risankizumab C_{avg} quartiles from Week 0 to Week 16. (**Figure 3**).
L337: **Figure 3** Exposure-Response for Safety by Risankizumab C_{avg} Quartiles Over Week 0-16
L338: (Source of data: Exposure Response Report R&amp;D/17/0883, Figure 11)
L339: ## 6.3.2.3 Is an alternative dosing regimen or management strategy required for subpopulations based on intrinsic factors?
L340: No, an alternative dosing regimen or management strategy is not necessary for subpopulations based on intrinsic factors. Population PK analysis identified body weight (BW) as a significant covariate on risankizumab PK; however, a dose adjustment based on BW is not necessary based on the available efficacy data from the Phase 3 trials and exposure-response analyses.
L341: According to the review team's E-R analysis results, model-predicted response rates for PASI 75, PASI 90 and PASI 100 at Week 16 are 3 - 7% lower in subjects with body weight &gt;100 kg than in subjects with body weight \le100 kg at the proposed dosing regimen (Table 5). The Applicant's model-based E-R simulations indicate that the proposed risankizumab dosing regimen has achieved a plateau of efficacy with regard to PASI 90 and sPGA 0/1, regardless of body weight \le100 kg or &gt;100 kg (Figure 4).

L349: ## **6.3.2.4** *Are there clinically relevant drug-drug interactions and what is the appropriate management strategy?*
L350: No, risankizumab has no clinically meaningful effect on the PK of drugs metabolized by cytochrome P450 (CYP)1A2, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, or CYP3A.
L351: The Applicant conducted a clinical drug interaction study (M16-007 [1311.36]) to evaluate the potential effect of risankizumab, 150 mg administered SC Q4W x 4, on the activities of CYP1A2 (caffeine), CYP2C9 (warfarin), CYP2C19 (omeprazole), CYP2D6 (metoprolol) and CYP3A(midazolam) using a cocktail approach in subjects with moderate to severe psoriasis. A single oral dose of this cocktail containing these five CYP probe substrates was administered on Day 1 (before risankizumab treatment) and Day 98 (after risankizumab treatment). The PK results showed that the 90% confidence intervals (CI) for the ratios of the probe substrates Cmax and AUCinf before and after risankizumab treatment were within the no-effect boundaries of 0.8 to 1.25, except for the lower 90% confidence bound of omeprazole Cmax ratio of 0.73. See Section 13.5.2.3 of this multi-discipline review for detailed information.

L374: ## 7.2. Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
L375: ## 7.2.1. Pivotal Phase 3 Trials (M15-995 [1311.28], M16-008 [1311.3])
L376: ## 7.2.1.1 Study Design and Endpoints
L377: The applicant conducted two pivotal Phase 3 trials (M15-995 [1311.28], M16-008 [1311.3]) to support the application.
L378: For both trials, the key inclusion criteria that defined the study population were identical and are as follows:
L379: - Male or female 18 years of age or older
- Diagnosis of plaque psoriasis (with or without psoriatic arthritis) for at least 6 months before the first administration of study drug
- Stable moderate to severe chronic plaque-type psoriasis with or without psoriatic arthritis at both screening and baseline (randomization):Static Physician Global Assessment (sPGA) score of at least 3 (moderate), see Appendix 13.4 for details on the sPGA scale
- Psoriasis Area and Severity Index (PASI) \ge 12, see Appendix 13.4 for details on the calculation of PASI
- Body Surface Area (BSA) \ge 10\%
L380: Subjects with non-plaque forms of psoriasis (including guttate, erythrodermic, or pustular), or with current drug-induced psoriasis (including an exacerbation of psoriasis from beta blockers, calcium channel blockers, or lithium), or with active ongoing inflammatory diseases other than psoriasis and psoriatic arthritis that might confound trial evaluations according to investigator's judgement were excluded. Subjects who
L381: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – BLA 761105
## (Skyrizi, Risankizumab)
L382: were previously exposed to risankizumab (BI 655066) or ustekinumab (Stelara®) were also excluded.
L383: Figure 5 presents the study designs for Trials 1311.28 and 1311.3. Both were multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo and active-controlled (EU-sourced ustekinumab), parallel-group, Phase 3 trials evaluating the safety and efficacy of risankizumab for the treatment of moderate-to-severe plaque psoriasis. In both trials, the randomization was stratified by baseline body weight (\le 100 kg vs. &gt; 100 kg), and prior exposure to TNF antagonists (0 vs. \ge 1).
L384: Both trials consisted of the following periods: a screening period, a randomized treatment period (Week 0 through Week 40), and a follow-up period (Week 41 through Week 56).
L385: For the 40-week Randomized Treatment Period in each trial, the protocol specified randomizing approximately 500 subjects to one of the following treatment arms in a 3:1:1 ratio:
L386: - Risankizumab 150 mg subcutaneously (SC) at Weeks 0 and 4, then every 12 (Q12) weeks (300 subjects)
- Ustekinumab (Stelara) [45 mg for &lt; 100 kg and 90 mg for &gt; 100 kg weight] SC at Weeks 0 and 4, then Q12 weeks (100 subjects)
- Placebo SC at Weeks 0 and 4, then at Week 16 switch to risankizumab 150 mg SC Q12 weeks (100 subjects)
L387: Subjects were evaluated at the following study visits: screening, baseline (Day 1), Weeks 4, 8, 12, 16, 22, 28, 34, 40 (end of treatment), 46, 52, and 56 (follow-up visits 1, 2, and 3, respectively).

L392: - Proportion of subjects who achieve \ge 90\% reduction from baseline PASI (PASI 90) at Week 16

The new secondary endpoints added in the final SAPs include sPGA of 0 at Week 16, PASI 100 at 16, and sPGA 0 at Week 52. It should be noted that the secondary endpoints were tested in a hierarchical order to control the Type I error rate (\alpha=0.05) and were included in the prespecified multiplicity testing strategy.

L397: ## **Psoriasis Symptom Scale (PSS)**
L398: The PSS is a four-item patient reported outcome (PRO) instrument that assess the severity of psoriasis symptoms in patients with moderate to severe psoriasis. The symptoms included are pain, redness, itching, and burning from psoriasis. Current symptom severity is assessed as a daily diary, using a 5-point Likert-type scale ranging from 0(none) to 4 (very severe). According to the protocol, the PSS was developed based on published evidence supporting the development of two similar, proprietary PRO instruments, the Psoriasis Symptom Inventory (PSI) and the Psoriasis Symptom Diary (PSD).
L399: ## **7.2.1.2 Statistical Methodologies**
L400: The primary analysis population was the Intent to Treat (ITT) population defined as all randomized subjects. The SAPs specified conducting supportive analyses using the Per Protocol Set (PPS) defined as the following:
L401: - Subjects who received at least 75% of planned study drug injections (including dummy placebo injections).
- Subjects who had either a PASI or sPGA assessment post-baseline
- Subjects who had stable moderate to severe chronic plaque psoriasis at baseline (BSA\ge10%, PASI\ge12, sPGA\ge3)
- Subjects who had at least one post-baseline assessment for the corresponding endpoint.
L402: For the analysis of the co-primary endpoints and the binary secondary endpoints, the protocols specified using the Cochran Mantel Haenszel (CMH) test stratified by weight (\le100 kg vs. &gt;100 kg) and priori exposure to TNF antagonists (0 vs \ge1). For the analysis of the continuous endpoint (i.e., the mean change from baseline in PSS at Week 16), the protocol specified the van Elteren test.
L403: The protocols specified testing the endpoints in a hierarchical order as in Table 7 to control the Type I error rate (two-sided, \alpha=0.05), and only if the two co-primary endpoints are statistically significant, the secondary endpoints would be tested.
L404: For handling of missing data for the binary endpoints, the protocols specified using No Response Imputation (NRI) approach and consider the missing data as failures. The protocols specified that only if visits before and after the missing are available and are both successes, then the missing data will be considered as a success, otherwise, NRI will be used. Furthermore, the protocols specified that subjects that take prohibited medications to treat psoriasis will be considered as discontinuing the trial, and will be considered as failures for the binary endpoints. Forsensitivity analyses for handling missing data of the binary endpoints, the protocols specified using last observation
L405: carried forward (LOCF), logistic regression, and multiple imputation (MI). For the continuous PSS endpoint, the protocols specified using LOCF as the primary imputation method, and the mixed model for repeated measures (MMRM) as a sensitivity analysis method.
L406: ## 7.2.1.3 Subject Disposition, Demographics, and Baseline Disease Characteristics
L407: Trial 1311.28 randomized a total of 491 subjects from 65 centers. Trial 1311.3 randomized a total of 506 subjects from 79 centers. Table 8 presents the disposition of subjects during the initial treatment period (16 weeks) of Trials 1311.28 and 1311.3. The discontinuation rates were low in each trial (&lt;5%), and note that with such low rates of missing data along with the large treatment effect, the impact of the imputation method on efficacy is minimal.

L411: For both trials, the baseline demographics and baseline disease characteristics are presented in Table 9 and Table 10, respectively. The demographics and baseline disease characteristics were generally balanced across the treatment arms within each trial and were similar between the trials. Approximately 70% of the subjects were male, 78% of the subjects were White, 88% of subjects were 18-64 years of age, 72% of the subjects were ≤100 kg, 78% of the subjects had no history of prior TNF use, and 82% of the subjects had sPGA score of moderate (3) at baseline.
