L844: This SUR provides approximately 7 months of additional safety data from the data cutoff for the initial submission (01 September 2017) and includes all data up to 29 March 2018 (SUR data cutoff). The SUR includes data from 2,471 adult subjects (an additional 237 subjects since the initial submission) with moderate to severe plaque psoriasis who received at least 1 dose of risankizumab in Phase 2 and 3 clinical studies representing an additional 1185.0 PY of exposure for a total of 3351.6 PY of exposure.
L845: With additional safety data as of this SUR data cutoff, no new safety signals have been identified compared with the initial submission. Relevant data and/or cases from the SUR are discussed within the review section that pertains to the safety issue. The additional data provided in the SUR supports the conclusions from the integrated safety analyses.
L846: ## Additional Safety Explorations
L847: ## Human Reproduction and Pregnancy
L848: In cynomolgus monkeys, a dose-dependent increase in fetal/infant loss was noted in the risankizumab-treated groups compared to the vehicle control group. The percent fetal/infant loss was 19%, 32%, and 43% in the vehicle control, 5 mg/kg, and 50 mg/kg groups, respectively.
L849: In the risankizumab psoriasis clinical development program, male subjects and their female partners were not required as per the protocols to use contraception. It is not expected that large molecule proteins would interact directly with DNA or other chromosomal material and the amount of risankizumb exposure to female partners transferred via seminal fluid is likely to be negligible.
L850: Nine paternal exposure pregnancies were reported in the partner of a male study subject in the risankizumab clinical development program, no congenital anomalies were reported as outcome.
L851: Female subjects of childbearing potential were required to use a highly effective method of birth control (that result in a low failure rate of less than 1% per year). Negative pregnancy tests were required at screening/baseline and urine pregnancy testing was performed at appropriate intervals. Subjects who became pregnant were to withdraw from treatment and were followed until delivery and outcome was reported. Pregnancy in a study subject was not considered an AE.
L852: Seventeen maternal exposure pregnancies occurred in the risankizumab clinical development program, of which 14 occurred in studies for the indication of psoriasis and 3 occurred in studies for other indications. Pregnancy outcome by treatment arm are shown in Table 35.
L853: **Table 35: Summary of Pregnancy Outcomes in Risankizumab Clinical Program**
L854: Maternal Exposure Outcomes (N = 17)	Risankizumab	Comparator	Placebo	Total
Live birth without congenital anomaly	3	0	0	3
Spontaneous Abortion	2	2	0	4
Elective termination (no fetal defects or unknown)	5	1	0	6
Ongoing pregnancy	4	0	0	4
Total	14	3	0	17
L855: Source: Applicant's SCS Table 41
L856: Two pregnancies in which the mother was taking risankizumab occurred since the initial submission. Both cases occurred in subjects with Crohn's disease (CD); 1 pregnancy is ongoing and 1 was an elective termination due to fetal defects cystic hygroma and fetal hydrops. The case is as follows:
L857: A 24-year-old white female with CD who was exposed to risankizumab for approximately 18 months including prior to conception and during the first trimester elected for termination of the pregnancy at 12 weeks due to a fetus with cystic hygroma and fetal hydrops. Concomitant medications included nortriptyline, paracetamol, and desogestrel. Chromosomal analysis from chorionic villuss ampling was normal and human parvovirus testing was not suggestive of an acute infection. The Investigator considered the cystic hygroma and fetal hydrops to have a reasonable possibility of being related to study drug; however, AbbVie considered the causality to be not related due to the following:
L858: Transfer of immunoglobulins across the placental syncytiotrophoblast requires the neonatal Fc receptor, which is barely detectable before gestational Week 14. Therefore, immunoglobulin G (IgG) placental transfer is minimal in the first trimester, hence limiting large molecule drug transfer to the fetus during the period of organogenesis (between gestational Weeks 3 and 8).
L859: The Fc neonatal receptor (FcRn) has been demonstrated to play a critical role in mediating IgG transplacental transfer, but recent studies demonstrating distinct transfer efficiencies of different epitope specific-IgG suggest that other mechanisms could also contribute to the regulation of IgG transfer.17 IgG can be detected in cord blood as early as 8–10 weeks of gestation.18 IgG1 is the most efficiently transported subclass and
L860: 17 Fouda G. et al. The Impact of IgG transplacental transfer on early life immunity. Immunohorizons. 2018 Jan 1; 2(1): 14–25.
L861: 18 Simister NE. Placental transport of immunoglobulin G. Vaccine. 2003;21:3365–3369.
L862: efficiency of IgG transfer can vary from one antigen-specificity to another. Most of the information on maternal-fetal transfer comes from measurements of the concentrations of endogenous antibodies. It is unknown how efficiently risankizumab, an exogenous IgG1 antibody would transfer to the fetal circulation at therapeutic concentrations.
L863: Cystic hygromas can occur as an isolated finding or in association with other birth defect as part of a syndrome. They result from environmental factors, genetic factors, or unknown factors. Chromosome abnormality does not appear to be a factor in this case. It is not stated whether viral studies other than parvovirus were done. While there are a number of possible factors (other viral infections, chemical exposures) that could be causal but are difficult to attribute, exposure to risankizumab cannot be ruled out.
L864: One single case in the clinical development is not sufficient to assess a causal relationship between risankizumab and cystic hygromas with fetal hydrops. Because the available data are insufficient to inform risankizumab-associated risk in pregnant women, we will require that the applicant conduct: 1) a prospective, registry based observational exposure cohort study that compares the maternal, fetal, and infant outcomes of women exposed to risankizumab during pregnancy to an unexposed control population, and 2) a retrospective cohort study using claims or electronic medical record data or a case control study to assess adverse pregnancy, neonatal and infant outcomes of women exposed to risankizumab during pregnancy compared to an unexposed control population. Refer to Section 12 Postmarketing Requirements and Commitments for further details.
L865: ## Human Carcinogenicity or Tumor Development
L866: There is a theoretical risk of increased malignancy for any product that produces immunosuppression and which is indicated for chronic administration. In patients with psoriasis, this risk may be potentiated by prior exposure to other immunosuppressive agents or other therapies that may enhance tumor development such as phototherapy.
L867: No genetic toxicology or carcinogenicity animal studies were conducted with risankizumab. The applicant provided a carcinogenic risk assessment, mainly using information from published literature. Refer to Section 5.1 for Pharmacology /Toxicology.
L868: The limited duration of observation during the drug development program is unlikely to allow detection of rare events with a long latency periods such as malignancy. Therefore, postmarketing data are needed to evaluate the long-term risk of malignancy in patients with psoriasis receiving risankizumab.
L869: ## Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth
L870: The applicant has not conducted an evaluation of the safety and efficacy of risankizumab in the pediatric population. Because the proposed product is a new active
L871: ingredient, approval of risankizumab for the treatment of moderate to severe plaque psoriasis in adult patients who are candidates for systemic therapy or phototherapy triggers the Pediatric Research Equity Act (PREA)(21 U.S.C. 355c). Per the Food and Drug Administration Safety and Innovation Act (FDASIA), the applicant submitted an initial Pediatric Study Plan (iPSP) on 8/19/2015. The Agency agreed with the iPSP (Agreement Letter dated 3/9/2016).
L872: In this submission, the applicant requested a partial waiver for studies in the pediatric population from birth to less than 6 years of age as “Necessary studies are impossible or highly impracticable due to the extremely low prevalence of moderate to severe psoriasis in this population.” (Section 505B (a)(4)(B)(i) of the Act). Although the epidemiologic data are limited regarding the pediatric population with moderate to severe plaque psoriasis, in general the prevalence of plaque psoriasis increases with age. In addition, the sponsor requested a deferral of studies in the pediatric population from age 6 years to &lt;18 years “until safety and efficacy in the adult indication has been demonstrated in Phase 3 studies” (Section 505B (a)(3)(A)(ii) of the Act).
L873: In the discussion with PeRC (3/20/2019) regarding the evaluation of risankizumab in the pediatric population, the Division conveyed their agreement with the proposed pediatric development program in the Agreed iPSP and the proposed timeline. PeRC concurred with the Division and recommended requesting earlier submission dates as per the iPSP. Refer to Section 12 for the pediatric study requirement for risankizumab under PREA.
L874: ## Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal, and Rebound
L875: No cases of risankizumab overdose has been reported. In the Crohn's disease Study M15-993 (1311.6), multiple doses of up to risankizumab 600 mg IV were administered and in the PK Study M16-513 (1311.16), doses of up to risankizumab 1200 mg IV were administered.
L876: There is no data to support an association of monoclonal antibodies including risankizumab with the potential for drug abuse. Therefore, the applicant did not evaluate abuse potential.
L877: The safety of risankizumab in subjects who had treatment withdrawn and restarted was assessed in the Phase 3 Study M15-992 and Phase 3 OLE Study M15-997. From the available data in these studies, no clinically meaningful changes in AEs were observed with subsequent retreatment. There were also no cases of rebound (defined as \ge 25\% increase in PASI relative to baseline within 60 days after re-randomization) in subjects who had risankizumab treatment withdrawn.
L878: ## 7.3.8. Safety in the Postmarket Setting
L879: ## Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L880: Risankizumab has not been approved for marketing in any country.
L881: ## Expectations on Safety in the Postmarket Setting
L882: There are no safety concerns that are expected to change the favorable risk/benefit assessment or lead to increased risk with administration of risankizumab in the postmarket setting. However, additional data are needed to characterize the safety profile of the proposed product in special populations (pregnant and lactating females and the pediatric population age \ge 6 years) and assess the risk of adverse events associated with long latency periods (malignancy) assess the risk of adverse events associated with long latency periods (malignancy). Refer to Section 12 of this review for the postmarketing requirements and commitments.
L883: ## 7.3.9. Integrated Assessment of Safety
L884: The safety profile for risankizumab was adequately characterized during the drug development program. The primary review of safety of risankizumab for the treatment of moderate to severe plaque psoriasis focused on the evaluation of pooled data from Phase 2 Trial 1311.2 and Phase 3 Trials M15-995 (1311.28), M16-008 (1311.3), M16-010 (1311.30) and M15-992 (1311.4). Trials include 1,590 subjects treated with risankizumab at the proposed dose of 150 mg SC. Of these, 1,091 subjects were exposed for at least 12 months, 96 subjects for 24 months, and 30 subjects for 36 months.
L885: Treatment with risankizumab did not appear to increase the risk of mortality. There were 5 deaths among subjects who received 150 mg risankizumab in the first 52 weeks of controlled trials and additional 5 deaths reported as occurring during the ongoing OLE trials (up through the safety update). None of the deaths can be assessed as causally related to the study product.
L886: During the Placebo-controlled period of the pooled trials, SAEs occurred in 2.4% of subjects treated with risankizumab compared to 4% of subjects treated with placebo. SAEs in the risankizumab group leading to discontinuation of study drug were cardiac failure congestive, drug-induced liver injury, liver injury, invasive lobular breast carcinoma, and esophageal carcinoma. The SAEs assessed by the investigators as possibly related to study drug were drug-induced liver injury, liver injury, cellulitis, herpes zoster, osteomyelitis, and asthma.
L887: Because of the mechanism of action of risankizumab, malignancy is a potential risk. After 52 weeks of treatment 3 subjects (0.5 E/100 PY) reported non-NMSC malignancies. For the overall development program, a total of 21 malignancies (excluding NMSC) were reported, 0.62 E/100 PY. The most frequent non-NMSC malignant tumors were breast cancer in 7 subjects, prostate cancer in 3 subjects and malignant melanoma in 2 subjects. For NMSC malignancies the ratio of SCC to BCC was approximately 1:1.5 for the 25 NMSC malignancies reported. Although the event rate for malignancy is slightly higher than other sources of data in psoriasis patients, there was only one death from malignancy in the risankizumab development program. At this juncture a causal link between the product and malignancy is not evident. Additional long-term data is needed to characterize the risk of malignancy.
L888: Infections (including serious infections, TB, opportunistic infections, fungal infections, and herpes zoster) are a safety concern because of the mechanism of action of risankizumab. Compared to placebo, subjects treated with risankizumab experienced more infections, including a higher rate of fungal infections (mostly tinea). Infections and infestations is the SOC with the highest number of subjects with SAE in the safety set for all risankizumab treated subjects. Two subjects discontinued due to serious infections (1 due to sepsis and pneumonia and 1 due to pneumonia). Over the entire psoriasis program, the rates of opportunistic infections among risankizumab-treated subjects with psoriasis were low (0.3 E/100 PY). None of the opportunistic infections were serious or led to discontinuation of study drug. Latent TB was reported in 7 subjects and 2 subjects reported a positive test for *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* complex. There were no reports of active TB. Subjects with active TB were excluded and those with latent TB were required to start treatment prophylaxis prior to starting on risankizumab. Language pertaining to infections and pre-treatment for tuberculosis is recommended to be included in Section 5.3 (Warnings and Precautions) of risankizumab labeling.
L889: Treatment with risankizumab was not associated with an increased incidence of treatment-related adverse reactions in the categories of suicidal ideation and behavior (SIB) and major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE). In the “other CV events” category of the ustekinumab-controlled trials, there appeared to be an imbalance (i.e. 4 events of arrhythmia and 4 events of CHF in the risankizumab arms vs 0 events in the ustekinumab arms) that occurred in 7 patients. In the absence of imbalances in the larger sample size of the primary pooled safety population, this low-incidence observation may have been due to chance. Because there is a lack of serious hypersensitivity cases and the cases anaphylaxis are confounded, hypersensitivity reactions will not be added to sections 4 and 5 of product labeling. Overall, 6 subjects (0.2%) discontinued treatment due to hepatic events. Some serious hepatic events occurred. There were no cases of fulminant hepatic failure. Most of the events were confounded with concomitant medication or comorbid conditions. It is recommended that the applicant conduct a long-term observational post-marketing study that assesses outcomes that include, but are not limited to, serious infections, tuberculosis, opportunistic infections, hypersensitivity reactions, autoimmune disease, neurologic or demyelinating disease, cardiovascular, gastrointestinal and hematologic adverse events.
L890: During the placebo-controlled period, the most common adverse reactions (AR) in subjects treated with risankizumab were upper respiratory infections (10.1%), fatigue (2.2%), pruritis (1.5%), cough (1.2%), urinary tract infection (1.2%), and nausea (1.1%). The frequency of AR was comparable across all age and demographic groups. These AR are recommended to be included in Section 6 (Adverse Reactions) of risankizumab labeling.
L891: Among subjects receiving risankizumab in the entire development program there were 19 maternal exposure pregnancies. Pregnancy outcomes included a single case of a
L892: fetus with cystic hygroma and fetal hydrops and are described in section 7.3.8 of this review. Because the available data are insufficient to inform risankizumab-associated risk in pregnant women, we will require post-approval prospective and retrospective studies assessing maternal, fetal and neonatal outcomes of women exposed to risankizumab during pregnancy compared to an unexposed control population. Refer to Section 12 Postmarketing Requirements and Commitments for further details.
L893: The safety data currently available demonstrate that risankizumab is safe for the treatment of moderate to severe plaque psoriasis in adult patients who are candidates for systemic therapy or phototherapy. Postmarketing risk management will include professional labeling, prescription status and routine pharmacovigilance. The long-term risk of malignancy will be assessed by a long-term observational study. The maternal, fetal, and infant outcomes of women exposed to risankizumab during pregnancy will be evaluated by a registry based observational exposure cohort study.
L894: ## 7.4. Summary and Conclusions
L895: ## 7.4.1. Statistical Issues
L896: There were no major statistical issues affecting overall conclusions. The treatment effects were large and consistent across trials and endpoints. The amount of missing data was relatively small (&lt;5%) at the primary timepoint, Week 16. Given the relatively large treatment effect along with the relatively small amount of missing data, the impact of the choice of an imputation method was minimal. The results for each of co-primary efficacy endpoint for each of the pivotal trials was statistically significant (p-values &lt; 0.001).
L897: There were no substantial differences in the treatment effect among subgroups. For the pivotal Phase 3 trials, approximately 70% of the subjects were male, 78% of the subjects were White, 88% of subjects were 18-64 years of age, 72% of the subjects were ≤100 kg, 78% of the subjects had no history of prior TNF use, and 82% of the subjects had sPGA score of moderate (3) at baseline. Given the relatively small number of subjects in the complement of the respective subgroups listed above, estimation of treatment effects in these subgroups may not be reliable.
L898: ## 7.4.2. Conclusions and Recommendations
L899: To establish the effectiveness of risankizumab, the applicant submitted results from two randomized, multicenter, placebo- and ustekinumab-controlled, parallel-group, Phase 3 trials (Trials 1311.28 and 1311.3). The trials enrolled subjects at least 18 years of age with moderate to severe psoriasis (i.e., sPGA score of at least 3 (moderate), PASI \ge12, and BSA \ge10%) at baseline. The co-primary efficacy endpoints were the proportion of subjects achieving a sPGA score of 0 or 1, with at least 2-grade improvement from baseline, at Week 16, and the proportion of subjects achieving PASI 90 at Week 16.
L900: In both trials, risankizumab was statistically superior to placebo (p-values &lt;0.001) for both co-primary efficacy endpoints at Week 16. Although the applicant also showed that risankizumab was statistically superior to ustekinumab at Week 16 (p-values&lt;0.001), the applicant did not provide an adequate scientific bridge between the US-licensed and the EU-approved ustekinumab. Thus, the EU-approved ustekinumab may be considered distinct from the US-licensed ustekinumab.
L901: The applicant provided adequate safety exposures to conclude a risk/benefit analysis is sufficient for approval of the application. The summary of the safety information includes:
L902: - Treatment with risankizumab did not appear to increase the risk of mortality as compared to placebo through 16 weeks of randomized, blinded treatment.
- A limited number of serious adverse events (SAEs) in the risankizumab group led to discontinuation of study drug.
- An association between the product and malignancy was not observed, given the limited clinical experience to date.
- Treatment with risankizumab does not appear to be associated with an increased incidence of treatment-related adverse suicidal ideation and behavior (SIB)
- Treatment with risankizumab does not appear to be associated with an increased incidence of treatment-related major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE).
- Serious cases of hypersensitivity and injection site reactions did not occur.
- Some serious non-MACE cardiovascular and hepatic events occurred. Most of the events were confounded with concomitant medication or comorbid conditions.
- Compared to placebo, subjects treated with risankizumab experienced more infections, more serious infections and a higher rate of fungal infections. There were no reports of active TB.
- The available data are insufficient to inform risankizumab-associated risk in pregnant women and children.
- There is limited safety data beyond one year for the use of risankizumab 150 mg subcutaneous injection.
L903: Based on the available information, the safety profile appears to be similar to the premarket safety database for guselkumab and tildrakizumab, which are approved
L904: human monoclonal IgG1 antibodies that target the p19 subunit of IL-23.
L905: ## 8 Advisory Committee Meeting and Other External Consultations
L906: The Agency held no Advisory Committee Meeting regarding this application because the safety profile was expected to be comparable to that of other biologic products approved for this indication. There were no novel or complex regulatory issues that required public discussion at an AC meeting.

L924: ## 12 Postmarketing Requirements and Commitments
L925: Clinical postmarketing requirements are intended to characterize the risks of risankizumab use in special populations (pregnancy) and address the long-term safety of this novel biologic product in the target population. Development of final pharmacovigilance plans is ongoing.
L926: The available safety data regarding risankizumab use during pregnancy are limited. The study population as defined by the entry criteria excluded pregnant and breastfeeding females, and females planning to become pregnant or breastfeed during the trials. Because exposures to risankizumab during pregnancy are likely to occur and the available data are insufficient to characterize the associated risk in pregnant women, the Agency will require the applicant to conduct the post-marketing assessments to characterize the drug associated risk.
L927: Analysis of expected adverse reactions based on biologic plausibility and potential class effects did not identify a definitive safety signal. However, based on the mechanism of action, the Agency will require post-marketing safety studies to assess the risk of malignancy and other serious adverse events which may not be identified during clinical trials due to a long latency period or low incidence rate.
L928: **Based on review of the data in this submission, the following postmarketing requirements (PMRs) were** conveyed to the applicant:
L929: ## POSTMARKETING REQUIREMENTS UNDER 505(o)
L930: ## PMR 1:
L931: - A prospective, registry based observational exposure cohort study that compares the maternal, fetal, and infant outcomes of women exposed to risankizumab during pregnancy to an unexposed control population. The registry will detect and record major and minor congenital malformations, spontaneous abortions, stillbirths, elective terminations, small for gestational age, and any other adverse pregnancy outcomes. These outcomes will be assessed throughout pregnancy. Infant outcomes, including neonatal deaths, infections in the first 6 months of life, and effects on postnatal growth and development, will be assessed through at least the first year of life.
L932: **Draft Protocol Submission:**
L933: **Final Protocol Submission:**
L934: **Trial Completion:**
L935: **Final Report Submission:**
L936: Rationale: Moderate to severe psoriasis occurs in women of child bearing age. Therefore, we expect there will be some exposure of pregnant women to risankizumab. Data on use of risankizumab in pregnant women are needed.
L937: ## PMR 2:
L938: - Conduct a retrospective cohort study using claims or electronic medical record data or a case control study to assess adverse pregnancy outcomes such as major congenital malformations, spontaneous abortions, stillbirths, small for gestational age, neonatal
L939: deaths, and infant infections in women exposed to risankizumab during pregnancy compared to an unexposed control population.
L940: **Draft Protocol Submission:**
**Final Protocol Submission:**
**Trial Completion:**
**Final Report Submission:**
L941: Rationale: Trials in adults with moderate-to-severe plaque psoriasis who are candidates for systemic therapy or phototherapy are completed and the product is ready for approval. Pregnant women were excluded from the development program and data are needed in this population.
L942: ## **PMR 3:**
L943: - Conduct an observational study to assess the long-term safety of risankizumab compared to other therapies used in the treatment of adults with moderate-to-severe plaque psoriasis who are candidates for systemic therapy or phototherapy in the course of actual clinical care. The study's primary outcome is long-term malignancy. Secondary outcomes include, but are not limited to, serious infections, tuberculosis, opportunistic infections, hypersensitivity reactions, autoimmune disease, neurologic or demyelinating disease, cardiovascular, gastrointestinal and hematologic adverse events.
L944: Describe and justify the choice of appropriate comparator population(s) and estimated background rate(s) relative to risankizumab-exposed patients; clearly define the primary comparator population for the primary objective. Design the study around a testable hypothesis to assess, with sufficient sample size and power, a clinically meaningful increase in malignancy risk above the comparator background rate(s), with a prespecified statistical analysis method. Specify concise case definitions and validation algorithms for both primary and secondary outcomes. For the risankizumab-exposed and comparator(s) cohorts, clearly define the study drug initiation period and any exclusion and inclusion criteria. Enroll patients over an initial 4 year period and follow for a minimum of 8 years from the time of enrollment.

**Study Completion:**
**Final Report Submission:**
L946: Rationale: There is a concern that this new biologic product may increase the risk of malignancies and serious infections due to its immunosuppressive effect. This is an observational study to collect additional data on long-term safety.

L950: In accordance with 21 CFR 314.55(c)(3)(ii), the applicant, AbbVie, requests a partial waiver of pediatric studies with risankizumab for plaque psoriasis patients younger than 6 years of age. We are waiving the pediatric study requirement for ages 0 to less than 6 years because necessary studies are impossible or highly impracticable. This is because:
L951: - The prevalence of psoriasis in the 0 to less than 6 years age group is low (with the highest prevalence published of 0.3%) and the proportion of children with a severe condition in need of a systemic treatment is 4%, giving a final prevalence of the condition to be about 1 per 10,000 in this age group.
L952: In accordance with 21 CFR 314.55(b), the applicant, AbbVie, requests deferral of pediatric studies with risankizumab for the proposed plaque psoriasis indication in 6 to &lt; 18-year-old patients. We are deferring submission of pediatric studies for ages 6 years to less than 18 years for this application because this product is ready for approval for use in adults and the pediatric studies have not been completed.
L953: A planned study in the pediatric population age 6 years to less than 18 years of age was included in the Agreed iPSB and will be deferred. The applicant is proposing to conduct a single clinical trial (1311.PD) for the pediatric investigation plan to investigate the PK, safety and efficacy of risankizumab in pediatric subjects 6 to &lt; 18 years of age with moderate to severe disease

L992: ## HIGHLIGHTS
L993: ## INDICATIONS AND USAGE
L994: TRADENAME SKYRIZI is an interleukin-23 (b) (4) antagonist indicated for the treatment of moderate to severe plaque psoriasis in adults.
L995: ## 8.1 Pregnancy
L996: ## Risk Summary
L997: The limited data available with SKYRIZI use in pregnant women are insufficient to evaluate (b) (4) drug-associated risk(b) of major birth defects, miscarriage, or adverse maternal or fetal outcomes. Human IgG is known to cross the placental barrier; therefore, SKYRIZI may be transmitted from the mother to the developing fetus.
L998: In a(b) (4) enhanced pre- and post-natal developmental toxicity study, (b) (4) pregnant cynomolgus monkeys were administered subcutaneous doses of 5 and 50 mg/kg risankizumab (b) (4) once weekly during the period of organogenesis up to parturition. Increased fetal/infant loss was noted in pregnant monkeys treated with 50 mg/kg (see *Data*), which is 20 times (based on mg/kg comparison) the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD; 2.5 mg/kg based on administration of a 150 mg dose to a 60 kg individual).(b) (4)
L999: All pregnancies have a background risk of birth defect, loss, or other adverse outcomes. The estimated background risk of major birth defects and miscarriage for the indicated population is unknown. In the U.S. general population, the estimated background risk of major birth defects and miscarriage in clinically recognized pregnancies is 2% to 4% and 15% to 20%, respectively.
L1000: ## Data
L1001: ## Animal Data
L1002: An enhanced pre- and post-natal developmental toxicity study was conducted in cynomolgus monkeys. Pregnant cynomolgus monkeys were administered weekly
L1003: subcutaneous doses of risankizumab at 5 and 50 mg/kg from gestation day 20 to parturition and the cynomolgus monkeys (mothers and infants) were monitored for 6 months after delivery.
L1004: No maternal toxicity was noted in this study. There were no treatment-related effects on growth and development, malformations, developmental immunotoxicology or neurobehavioral development. However, a dose-dependent increase in fetal/infant loss was noted in the risankizumab-treated groups (32% and 43% in the 5 mg/kg and 50 mg/kg groups, respectively) compared to the vehicle control group (19%). The increased fetal/infant loss in the 50 mg/kg group was considered to be related to risankizumab treatment. The no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) for maternal toxicity was identified as 50 mg/kg (20 times the MRHD, based on mg/kg comparison) and the NOAEL for developmental toxicity was identified as 5 mg/kg (2 times the MRHD, based on mg/kg comparison). In the infants, mean serum concentrations increased in a dose-dependent manner and were approximately 17-86% of the respective maternal concentrations. Following delivery, most adult female cynomolgus monkeys and all infants from the risankizumab-treated groups had measurable serum concentrations of risankizumab up to 91 days postpartum. Serum concentrations were below detectable levels at 180 days postpartum.
L1005: ## 12.1 Mechanism of Action
L1006: Risankizumab is a humanized immunoglobulin G1 (IgG1) monoclonal antibody that selectively binds to the p19 subunit of human interleukin 23 (IL-23) cytokine and inhibits its interaction with the IL-23 receptor. IL-23 is a naturally occurring cytokine that is involved in inflammatory and immune responses.
L1007: Risankizumab inhibits the release of proinflammatory cytokines and chemokines.

L1015: ## Static Physician Global Assessment (sPGA) scale
L1016: Score	Short description	Detailed description
0	clear	No signs of psoriasis. Post-inflammatory hyperpigmentation may be present
1	almost clear	Normal to pink coloration
Just detectable (possible slight elevation above normal skin)
No to minimal focal scaling
2	mild	Pink to light red coloration
Mild thickening (slight but definite elevation, typically edges are indistinct or sloped)
Predominantly fine scaling
3	moderate	Dull to bright red coloration
Clearly distinguishable to moderate thickening
Moderate scaling
4	severe	Bright to deep dark red coloration;
Severe thickening with hard edges
Severe coarse scaling covering almost all or all lesions
L1017: Source: Applicant's protocol
L1018: ## **Psoriasis Area Severity Index (PASI)**
L1019: The protocol specified that in order to calculate the PASI score, the four main body areas were assessed: head (h), trunk (t), upper extremities (u) and lower extremities (l). These corresponded to 10, 30, 20, and 40% of the total body area, respectively. The signs of severity, erythema (E), infiltration (I), and desquamation (D) of lesions were assessed using a 5-point numeric scale (0-4) where 0 is a complete lack of cutaneous involvement and 4 is the severest possible involvement; scores were made independent of each of the areas, h, t, u, and l and represented a composite score for each area. The PASI score was calculated according to the following formula:
L1020: \text{PASI} = 0.1(Eh+Ih+Dh)Ah + 0.3(Et+It+Dt)At + 0.2(Eu+Iu+Du)Au + 0.4(El+Il+Dl)Al

L1037: ## 13.5.1 Summary of Bioanalytical Method Validation and Performance
L1038: ## 13.5.1.1 PK assays: bioanalytical methods for determination of risankizumab concentrations in human plasma
L1039: The Applicant developed and validated an ELISA for measurement of risankizumab concentrations in human plasma samples from all clinical trials. The method is applicable for the quantitation of risankizumab within a nominal range of 5 to 100 ng/mL, with lower limit of quantitation (LLOQ) of 5 ng/mL. In the assay, ELISA plates are coated with a polyclonal anti-risankizumab antibody and nonspecific binding is prevented by the addition of blocking buffer. Captured risankizumab is bound by a biotinylated anti-idiotypic risankizumab antibody. The amount of bound biotinylated antibody is quantified with horse-radish peroxidase conjugated to streptavidin. By addition of the peroxidase substrate 3,3',5,5'-tetramethylbenzidine, an enzymatic reaction is initiated and the absorbance is measured at 450 nm using a SpectraMax Plate Reader after termination of the reaction. The validation parameters and performance of the ELISA are summarized in Table 40.

L1046: ## 13.5.1.2 Immunogenicity assays: bioanalytical methods for testing anti-drug antibodies and neutralizing anti-drug antibodies
L1047: The Applicant developed an ECL immunoassay for assessment of ADA in risankizumab clinical trials. In brief, human plasma samples are diluted 1:2 and treated with acetic acid, then neutralized with buffer containing biotinylated and Sulfo-Tag™ labeled assay reagents. Biotinylated-risankizumab and Sulfo-Tag™ labeled risankizumab are incubated to form an antibody complex bridge. After incubation, the samples are then pipetted onto a blocked MSD-Streptavidin plate, allowing unbound material to be washed away.
L1048: Only the samples that contain antibody bound to both the labeled assay reagents generate an ECL signal.
L1049: A cell-based NAb assay was developed and validated for measures NAb in human plasma through assessment of STAT3 phosphorylation. Plasma samples are incubated with a fixed concentration of IL-23. The human large B cell lymphoma cell line used in the assay displays increased phosphorylated STAT3 in the presence of IL-23. Antibodies that bind to and neutralize risankizumab increase IL-23-mediated IL-23R activation and consequent STAT3 phosphorylation. Phosphorylated-STAT3 is measured using the PathScan® Phospho-Stat3 (Tyr705) Sandwich ELISA.
L1050: ## ***Sensitivity and drug tolerance of the immunogenicity assays used in the Phase 3 trials***
L1051: The ECL bridging assay had drug tolerance level of 5 mcg/mL of risankizumab in plasma for detection of 0.70 ng/mL ADA (Table 41). The mean steady-state C_{\text{trough}} across all phase 3 studies at Weeks 16, 28, 40, and 52 after administration were 1.84 mcg/mL, 1.60 mcg/mL, 1.57 mcg/mL, and 1.52 mcg/mL, respectively, which were lower than the drug tolerance level of the assay (5 mcg/mL). These findings suggest that the ADA levels could be detected as low as 0.7 ng/mL at the proposed clinical dosing regimen.
L1052: The NAb assay had drug tolerance levels of 70 ng/mL or 875 ng/mL of risankizumab in plasma for detection of 250 ng/mL or 2 mcg/mL NAb, respectively (Table 41). The detection of NAb could be interfered with the presence of risankizumab in plasma because the C_{\text{trough}} of risankizumab was above the drug tolerance levels of the NAb assay.
L1053: Refer to the Product Quality Review for more information regarding the performance and validation of the immunogenicity assays.

L1059: ## 13.5.2 Clinical Pharmacology Studies
L1060: ## 13.5.2.1 Study 1311.1: Single Ascending Dose Study in Subjects with Moderate to Severe Plaque Psoriasis
L1061: Study 1311.1 was a randomized, double-blind, single-ascending dose PK study of risankizumab in male and female subjects with psoriasis (n=39) that consisted of two stages:
L1062: In stage 1, six single IV infusion groups at risankizumab doses of 0.01, 0.05, 0.25, 1, 3, and 5 mg/kg, with active : placebo randomization of 3:1 in each dose group.
L1063: In stage 2, two SC dose groups at risankizumab doses of 0.25 and 1 mg/kg with active : placebo randomization of 7:1 and 6:1, respectively.
L1064: The results of risankizumab PK parameters are summarized in Table 42. The observed absolute SC bioavailability of risankizumab for the Phase 1 DS supply was 73%.

L1070: ## 13.5.2.2 Study M16-513 (1311.16): Single Dose PK Study in Caucasian, Japanese and Chinese Healthy Subjects
L1071: Study M16-513 (1311.16) was a randomized, single-dose PK study of risankizumab (n=89) that consisted of two stages. Eight male subjects (6:2 active : placebo randomization), ages of 21 to 45 years, were enrolled in each dose group for each ethnicity.
L1072: In Stage 1: single rising risankizumab doses were administered via SC rout in three sequential groups (18, 90, and 300 mg) in Chinese and Japanese subjects and two sequential groups (18 and 300 mg) in Caucasian subjects.
L1073: In Stage 2: single rising risankizumab doses were administered via IV infusion in the three sequential groups (200, 600, and 1200 mg) in Japanese subjects.
L1074: The PK results (Table 43) indicated that risankizumab exposures expressed as C_{max} and AUC were 22% to 31% higher in Chinese and Japanese subjects compared to healthy Caucasians. After accounting for body weight, risankizumab exposure were comparable across ethnic groups.

L1079: ## 13.5.2.3 Study M16-007 (1311.36): A Clinical Drug Interaction Study in Subjects with Psoriasis
L1080: Study M16-007 (1311.36) was a Phase 1, open-label, one-sequence, drug interaction study in subjects with psoriasis (n=21) to evaluate potential effect of risankizumab on the CYP enzyme activities.
L1081: ## Study design
L1082: **Day 1:** Subjects received a single oral dose of a cocktail containing CYP probe substrates: CYP1A2 (caffeine 100 mg), CYP2C9 (warfarin 10 mg), CYP2C19 (omeprazole 20 mg), CYP2D6 (metoprolol 50 mg), and CYP3A (midazolam 2 mg).
L1083: **Days 8 to 92:** Subjects received a total of 4 doses of risankizumab 150 mg SC Q4W on Days 8, 36, 64, and 92.
L1084: **Day 98:** Subject received the second single dose of the CYP probe substrates cocktail.
L1085: Blood sample were collected during the study to evaluate the PK of CYP probe substrates and risankizumab.
L1086: ## Study population
L1087: The demographic data based on the 21 subjects are summarized below:
L1088: - Sex: 19 males (90%) and 2 females (10%)
- Age (median): 47 years (range: 31-66 years)
- Race: 20 White (95%) and 1 Asian (5%)
- Body weight (mean\pmSD): 88\pm19 kg
L1089: ## Pharmacokinetics of risankizumab
L1090: Risankizumab Ctrough values (mean\pmSD) are presented in Figure 15. Mean Ctrough prior to administration of the 2nd (Day 36), 3rd (Day 64), and 4th (Day 92) dose were 7.3, 10.6, and 12.0 mcg/mL, respectively. The Ctrough at Day 92 was approximately 6-fold higher
L1091: than the mean steady-state C_{\text{trough}} of risankizumab at the dosing regimen in the Phase 3 studies.
L1092: ## Effect of risankizumab on Pharmacokinetics of CYP probe substrates
L1093: The effect of risankizumab on the PK of caffeine (metabolized by CYP1A2 to form paraxanthine), s-warfarin (metabolized by CYP2C9), omeprazole (metabolized by CYP2C19 to form 5-hydroxy-omeprazole), metoprolol (metabolized by CYP2D6 to form \alpha-hydroxymetoprolol), and midazolam (metabolized by CYP3A to form 1-hydroxymidazolam) and their corresponding metabolites are summarized in Table 44 to Table 48. Overall, the PK results showed that risankizumab had no clinically meaningful effect on the activities of CYP1A2, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and CYP3A (Table 34).

L1125: ## 13.5.2.4 Study 1311.2: A Phase 2 Dose-Ranging Trial in Subjects with Psoriasis
L1126: Study 1311.2 was a randomized, parallel-arm, dose-ranging, multiple-dose, active comparator-controlled study in subjects with moderate to severe chronic plaque psoriasis.
L1127: ## *Study design*
L1128: - Risankizumab 18 mg SC, single dose at Week 0, n=43
- Risankizumab 90 mg SC, at Weeks 0, 4, and 16, n=41
- Risankizumab 180 mg SC, at Weeks 0, 4, and 16, n=42
- Ustekinumab 45 or 90 mg SC (based on body weight) at Weeks 0, 4, and 16, n=40
L1129: Risankizumab in general showed dose-proportional increase in exposure across 90 mg and 180 mg SC doses. Risankizumab median Tmax was 7 days across all SC doses and geometric mean terminal phase t1/2 was estimated to be 28 days following a single dose of 18 mg.
L1130: ## 13.5.2.5 Study M16-008 (1311.3), Study M15-992 (1311.4), Study M15-995 (1311.28), Study M16-010 (1311.30): Risankizumab PK in Phase 3 Trials in Subjects with Psoriasis

L1136: ## 13.5.3 Immunogenicity
L1137: ## 13.5.3.1 Immunogenicity incidences
L1138: The treatment-emergent anti-drug antibody (TE-ADA) and treatment-emergent neutralizing antibody (TE-NAb) incidences in three Phase 3 trials are summarized in Table 50. The median time to appearance of ADA was 16 weeks across studies. The incidences for NAb among TE-ADA+ subjects were 42% (103/243) and 57% (150/263) over the durations of 16 weeks and 52 weeks, respectively. A total of 16 of 1079 ADA evaluable subjects (1.5%), representing 6% of TE-ADA+ subjects (16 of 263), had ADA titer values of \ge 128 over the duration of 52 weeks.
L1139: **Table 50 Summary of Immunogenicity Incidences in Phase 3 Trials**
L1140: Risankizumab Phase 3 Study	Total
M16-008	M15-995	M16-010	M15-992
Week 0-16
Subjects (n)	297	296	295	400	1288
TE-ADA incidence n (%)	50 (20%)	46 (16%)	62 (21%)	77 (19%)	243 (19%)
TE-NAb incidence n (%)	23 (8%)	15 (5%)	27 (9%)	38 (9.5%)	103 (8%)
Week 0-52
Subjects (N)	301	298	299	181	1079
TE-ADA incidence n (%)	78 (26%)	64 (22%)	74 (25%)	47 (26%)	263 (24%)
TE-NAb incidence n (%)	44 (15%)	34 (11%)	43 (14%)	29 (16%)	150 (14%)
Definitions of subjects ADA status are summarized below
ADA evaluable subjects: Subjects had at least 1 reportable immunogenicity assessment during the study post baseline.
Treatment-emergent anti-drug antibody (TE-ADA) or ADA positive: Subjects had (1) ADA negative or missing assessment at baseline (prior to 1st risankizumab dose) and became ADA positive at one or more time points post baseline, or (2) ADA positive at baseline and showed a 4-fold (based on 2 dilution steps at the titer determination stage) or greater increase in titer values relative to baseline or a titer value of 2 or greater in at least one post-dose samples if the baseline titer value was less than 1 (in this case 4-fold increment over midpoint of 0.5 was used).
ADA Negative: All pre- and post-treatment samples were negative for the ADA assay
Treatment-emergent neutralizing antibody (TE-NAb): The confirmed ADA positive samples were also evaluated in the neutralizing antibody (NAb) assay to test for the presence of NAb.TE-NAb indicated TE-ADA positive and positive for NAb.
NAb Negative included 1) ADA negative and 2) TE-ADA positive and negative for NAb
L1141: (Source of data: Reviewer's summary based on Immunogenicity Report R&amp;D/17/1378, Table 2 and Table 3)
L1142: ## 13.5.2.2 Immunogenicity impact on pharmacokinetics
L1143: As shown in Figure 16, risankizumab trough plasma concentration central tendency and distribution by visit and ADA status using pooled data across all four Phase 3 trials were comparable between subjects who were ADA positive and those who were ADA
L1144: negative over time, indicating that ADA to risankizumab did not have a clinically relevant impact on PK of risankizumab in majority of ADA positive subjects.
L1145: In a small subset of subjects who developed ADA titer of \ge 128 (6% of ADA positive subjects), risankizumab clearance was estimated to be increase by 43%, resulting in 30% to 40% decrease risankizumab AUC, on average, compared to ADA negative subjects.
L1146: **Figure 16** Comparison of Risankizumab Plasma Concentrations by ADA Status

L1148: ## 13.5.2.3 Immunogenicity impact on efficacy
L1149: The comparison of PASI90 and sPGA0/1 responses at Week 16 and Week 52 by ADA or NAb status (positive or negative) using pooled data across Phase 3 studies are summarized in Table 51. The results indicate that ADA has no clinically relevant impact on efficacy.
L1150: A small subset of subjects (n=16) who had ADA titer of \ge 128 (1.5% of evaluated subjects) was associated with reduced drug concentrations (30%) and reduced efficacy (~50%) compared to ADA negative subjects.

L1155: ## 13.5.2.4 Impact of immunogenicity on safety
L1156: The incidence of injection site reactions, but not the incidence of hypersensitivity reactions, was numerically higher among ADA positive subjects than the ADA negative subjects in the primary safety pool analysis set (Week 0-16) and the all risankizumab psoriasis analysis set (Week 0-52) of Phase 3 safety results (Table 52).

L1160: ## 13.5.4 Population PK Analysis Results
L1161: A two-compartment pharmacokinetic model with first-order absorption adequately describes the pharmacokinetics of risankizumab. The parameter estimates for the final population PK model are presented in Table 53. Risankizumab clearance was estimated at 0.25 L/day. Risankizumab absolute bioavailability for the to-be-marketed drug product was estimated at 89%.
L1162: Between-subject variability (CV%) for clearance, central and peripheral volumes of distribution, and absorption rate were 39%, 69%, 39% and 47%, respectively. Current model residual variability was estimated at 26%, assuming a log normal distribution of this parameter.
L1163: The population PK analysis confirmed body weight is an important determinant of risankizumab clearance. The impact of age, sex or smoking status on PK was not deemed clinically meaningful (Table 53). The population PK analysis employed the full covariate model approach where all covariates of interest were evaluated as part of a single analysis run (full model). Figure 17 illustrates the covariate effects on risankizumab clearance in the final population PK model. The effect of body weight on CL was estimated as approximately linear with the power coefficient estimated at 1. Central and peripheral volumes were also found to increase with increasing body weight. The power coefficients for the effect of body weight were 1.25 and 0.52 for the central and peripheral volume of distribution, respectively.
L1164: The clearance for a 50 kg subject is predicted to be 67% of the clearance for a 75 kg subject used as a reference individual/population (0.25 L/day). For a 90 or 120 kg subject, the clearance is predicted to be 120% or 160% of the reference population value. A steeper effect of body weight is predicted on the central volume of distribution. Central volume of distribution is predicted to vary from 60% of its reference population value for a 50 kg individual up to 180% for an individual weighting 120 kg. Peripheral volume is predicted to increase less steeply; from 81% to 128% over the same range of body weight.
L1165: The population PK analysis results showed that the clearance in subjects who developed ADA titer \ge 128 was 43% higher than in subjects who did not develop ADA.
L1166: **Table 53: Structural (fixed effects) and variance (random effects) parameters of population PK model**

L1168: EST= estimate; RSE= relative standard error; SE= standard error, \theta fixed effects parameters, \omega^2 random effects parameters. Continuous covariates (AGE, BWT) of pharmacokinetic parameters were modeled using:
L1169: P_{TV} = \theta_x \left( \frac{R_1}{R_{ref}} \right)^{\theta_y}. Categorical covariate relationship was given by P_{TV} = \theta_x \cdot \theta_y^R. P_{TV} is a typical PK parameter, \theta_x, \theta_y, \theta_z are its fixed effect parameters, R_1 the continuous or R_2 the categorical covariate (0-1), and R_{ref} the reference value of a continuous covariate.

L1173: ## 13.5.5 Exposure-Response for Efficacy and Safety
L1174: ## Exposure-Response for Safety:
L1175: The relationships between model-predicted risankizumab plasma C_{avg} from Week 0 to 16 and up to 52 weeks (Week 0 to 52 from Studies M16-008, M15-995 and M15-992 and Week 0 – 44 from Study M16-010) and percentage of subjects who experienced any AE, SAE, infection and infestation, or serious infection, respectively, are presented in Figure 18 and Figure 19. Subjects were binned according to their individual plasma exposures into quartiles and the percent of subjects with the safety events noted above for each quartile were plotted.
L1176: **Figure 18: Exposure-Response Relationships Between Risankizumab Plasma Cavg and Safety Events of Interest Over the First 16 Weeks (0-16 Weeks)**
L1177: Median of average concentrations (Week 0 to 16) (µg/mL): Quartile 1 = 4.76 µg/mL, Quartile 2 = 6.57 µg/mL, Quartile 3 = 8.02 µg/mL, Quartile 4 = 10.13 µg/mL.
L1178: Source of data: Figure 11 of exposure response report
L1179: **Figure 19: Exposure-Response Relationships Between Risankizumab Plasma Cavg and Safety Events of Interest Over the First 52 Weeks (Week 0-52)**
L1180: Median of average concentrations (Week 0 to 52) (µg/mL): Quartile 1 = 2.98 µg/mL, Quartile 2 = 4.25 µg/mL, Quartile 3 = 5.87 µg/mL, Quartile 4 = 7.93 µg/mL.
L1181: Source of data: Figure 12 of exposure response report.
L1182: As shown in Figure 18 and Figure 19, there was no apparent relationship between risankizumab exposure and any AE, SAE, infection and infestation or serious infection over the first 16 weeks and up to 52 weeks treatment duration using pooled data from all four Phase 3 studies of risankizumab in subjects with moderate to severe plaque psoriasis.
L1183: In the placebo-controlled period (Week 0-16), the incidences of any AE, any SAE and any serious infection were comparable between placebo-treated and risankizumab-treated subjects. The incidence of any infection and infestation appeared to be slightly higher following risankizumab treatment compared to placebo treatment; however, no trend in exposure-response was observed among risankizumab treated subjects.

L1186: 54), in which 41 patients received 90mg dosing regimen, 42 patients received 180mg dosing regimen, and 1306 patients received 150mg dosing regimen.

L1213: ## A. EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
L1214: This Clinical Outcome Assessment (COA) review is provided as a response to a request for consultation by the Division of Dermatology and Dental Products (DDDP) regarding BLA 761105 for risankizumab. The proposed indication is for the treatment of moderate to severe plaque psoriasis in adults.
L1215: The Applicant used the following patient-reported outcome (PRO) assessments in their pivotal trials (Studies UltIMMa-1 (M16-008), UltIMMa-2 (M15-995), IMMvent, and IMMhance) in adult patients (\ge 18 years) with moderate to severe plaque psoriasis:

L1218: The Applicant proposes PRO-related labeling claims for Studies UltIMMa-1 and UltIMMa-2. The PRO efficacy assessment used in these studies as a key secondary endpoint was the PSS. Furthermore, PSS is adequate to support a labeling claim related to improvement in psoriasis signs and symptoms (i.e., pain, itching, redness, and burning). L1226: ## B. BACKGROUND
L1227: ## Materials Reviewed
L1228: - Current submission including PRO evidence dossier and draft product label
L1229: ## Disease background
L1230: Psoriasis is a chronic debilitating immunologic disease characterized by marked inflammation and thickening of the epidermis that result in thick, scaly plaques involving the skin.
L1231: While the majority of psoriasis patients are managed initially with topical therapies, those with severe and/or refractory disease may require phototherapy and/or systemic therapy. Oral systemic agents provide modest efficacy, but increasingly patients are treated with biological agents, such as TNF-alpha inhibitors (etanercept, adalimumab) and the p40 IL-12/23 inhibitor (ustekinumab).
L1232: ## Investigational product
L1233: Risankizumab is produced in a mammalian cell line using recombinant DNA technology. Risankizumab is a humanized immunoglobulin G1 (IgG1) monoclonal antibody that selectively binds with high affinity to the p19 subunit of human interleukin 23 (IL-23) cytokine and inhibits its interaction with the IL-23 receptor complex. IL-23 is a naturally occurring cytokine that is involved in inflammatory and immune responses. IL-23 supports the development, maintenance and activation of Th17 cells, which produces IL-17A, IL-17F, and IL-22, as well as other proinflammatory cytokines, and plays a key role in driving inflammatory autoimmune diseases, such as psoriasis. IL-23 is up-regulated in lesional skin in comparison to non-lesional skin of patients with plaque psoriasis. By blocking IL-23 from binding to its receptor, risankizumab inhibits IL-23 dependent cell signaling and release of proinflammatory cytokines. Risankizumab is administered as subcutaneous injection.
L1234: ## C. CLINICAL OUTCOME ASSESSMENT REVIEW
L1235: ## 1 CONTEXT OF USE
L1236: ## 1. Clinical Trial Population
L1237: The target population for Studies UltIMMA-1 and -2 were adults \ge 18 years of age with stable moderate to severe chronic plaque psoriasis of \ge 6 months duration; with or without PsA; body surface area (BSA) involvement \ge 10%; Psoriasis Area and Severity Index (PASI) \ge 12; Static Physician Global Assessment (sPGA) \ge 3; and candidates for systemic therapy or phototherapy.
L1238: For a complete list of inclusion and exclusion criteria, refer to the clinical study protocol.

L1240: Julia Ju, PharmD., PhD.

L1244: ## Reviewer's comment(s):
L1245: Overall, subjects had a median baseline PASI score of 17.8 and a median BSA of 20.0%.
L1246: Baseline sPGA score was severe in 19.3% of subjects. A total of 9.8% of study subjects had a history of diagnosed psoriatic arthritis.
L1247: ## 2. Clinical Trial Design
L1248: Studies UltIMMa-1 and UltIMMa-2 were replicate 52-week multi-national, randomized, double-blind, double-dummy, placebo and active comparator controlled, parallel design pivotal studies. The objective of both these studies were to assess the efficacy and safety of risankizumab compared to ustekinumab and placebo in patients with moderate to severe chronic plaque psoriasis. The study schema for Studies UltIMMa-1 and UltIMMa-2 is shown in Figure 1.
L1249: **Figure 1.** Study schema for Studies UltIMMa-1 and UltIMMa-2
L1250: Refer to the Clinical review for more details on the study design of Studies UltIMMa-1 and UltIMMa-2.
L1251: **Reviewer's comment(s):** The COA data was collected electronically via the use of electronic tablets (b) (4). Based on a recent site inspection, it has been identified that there are some data integrity issues regarding the use of the electronic tablets. Those issues include the following:
L1252: - The use of proxies for data entry without verification by a GRAPPA-certified assessor;
- The electronic devices recorded the incorrect date and time when the batteries for the (b) (4) were critically low;
- Loss of data due to synchronization errors caused by installation of software updates.
L1253: These data integrity issues may affect interpretation of COA data. The COA Staff defers to Clinical and Biostatistics whether the affected data should be excluded from all analyses.

L1261: **Reviewer's comment(s):** The PSS was utilized as a key secondary endpoint for risankizumab across Studies UltIMMa-1 and UltIMMa-2. The key evaluation of patient symptoms was performed at Week 16, where the risankizumab and placebo arms were compared by change in PSS total score as well as the proportion of subjects achieving a PSS total score of 0 (no itch, pain, burning, or redness).
L1262: Other PRO assessments were used in these trials (e.g., Dermatology Life Quality Index, EuroQoL Five-Dimension questionnaire, and Hospital Anxiety and Depression scale), but was not specified in the statistical hierarchy as a key secondary endpoint and is not further discussed in this review.

L1271: ## 3 CLINICAL OUTCOME ASSESSMENT
L1272: ## Psoriasis Symptom Scale (PSS)
L1273: The PSS is a 4-item PRO instrument designed to assess severity of four psoriasis signs and symptoms (pain, itching, redness, and burning). The PSS is a self-administered questionnaire in an electronic diary (e-Diary) format. Patients rate the severity of their symptoms 'over the past 24 hours' on a 5-point verbal rating scale ("0 = none" to "4 = very severe").
L1274: ## Clinical Outcome Assessment Review
L1275: Julia Ju, PharmD., PhD.
L1276: BLA 761105
L1277: Risankizumab
L1278: PSS, severity of psoriasis symptoms
L1279: **Reviewer's comment(s):** Patients in Studies ULTIMMA-1 and ULTIMMA-2 were asked to complete the PSS on an eDiary once per day in the evening within a specified time window from screening until the Week 16 visit. From Week 22 (visit 7) through Week 40 (EOT) patients completed the PSS during their clinic visit. The recall period was the same, last 24 hours.
L1280: ## 4 SCORING ALGORITHM
L1281: The PSS scores range from 0 (none) to 4 (very severe) with a total score ranging from 0 to 16. Higher scores indicate increased impairment (i.e., worse signs/symptoms).
L1282: ## 5 CONTENT VALIDITY
L1283: To date, the following information has been submitted (check all that apply):
L1284: -  Literature review and/or publications
-  Documentation of expert input
-  Qualitative study protocols and interview guides for focus group or patient interviews
-  Chronology of events for item generation, modification, and finalization (item tracking matrix)
-  Qualitative study summary with evidence to support item relevance, item stems and response options, and recall period
-  Qualitative support for meaningful change
-  Quantitative study summary with evidence to support item retention and scoring
-  Transcripts (if available)
L1285: The PSS was adapted from the literature and previously published psoriasis symptoms scales such as the Psoriasis Symptom Index (PSI) and the Psoriasis Symptom Diary (PSD), which were developed with extensive patient input. This adapted version was then evaluated for content validity in a qualitative study in moderate to severe plaque psoriasis patients.
L1286: Twenty (N=20) patients being treated for moderate to severe plaque psoriasis were interviewed to assess the content validity of the PSS via: 1) concept elicitation of psoriasis-related symptoms during which participants talk about their psoriasis symptom experience; and 2) cognitive debriefing of the PSS during which participants' comprehension and interpretation of the items, recall period and response options is assessed.
L1287: The 20 study participants had a mean age of 50.2 \pm 12.0 years (range: 25.0–73.0), and 55% were female. While all participants met the inclusion criteria of clinician-diagnosed moderate to severe plaque psoriasis, only 35% of the sample self-reported their psoriasis severity as moderate or severe. The Applicant noted that this discrepancy may be attributed to patients being well-controlled on their psoriasis treatment.
L1288: The average time since diagnosis of plaque psoriasis was almost 18 years, ranging from less than one to over 38 years. The most frequently reported symptoms and signs during the concept elicitation portion of the interviews included redness (n= 20, 100%), itching (n = 20, 100%), pain

L1294: (n = 15, 75%), burning (n = 13, 65%), and flaking (n = 11, 55%). Other psoriasis signs, symptoms or impacts described by participants included social impacts (n = 2, 10%), a feeling of inflammation or sunburn (n = 1, 5%), white spots (n = 1, 5%), and swollenness (n = 1, 5%). Most of these problems were mentioned in conjunction with other signs and symptoms.
L1295: Participants provided positive feedback on the PSS and felt it was comprehensive and relevant to their experience with psoriasis. Most participants understood the intended timeframe (n=17, 85%), meaning and response options (n=20,100%). No further changes were recommended to the PSS.
L1296: For more details on the qualitative research, refer to the PRO evidence dossier.
L1297: **Reviewer's comment(s):** The content validity of the PSS appears to be sufficiently established. Generally it is recommended to cautiously use "severe" and "very severe" response options as patients may not be able to distinguish between these response options. In the qualitative interviews, patients did not endorse the "very severe" response option. However, because the key secondary endpoint is proportion of patients achieving a score of 0 (resolution of psoriasis signs/symptoms), the concern for these response options are mitigated.
L1298: ## 6 OTHER MEASUREMENT PROPERTIES (RELIABILITY, CONSTRUCT VALIDITY, ABILITY TO DETECT CHANGE)
L1299: The measurement properties of the PSI were evaluated in the following two phase 3 studies: UltIMMa-1 (M16-008) and UltIMMa-2 (M15-995).
L1300: These two phase 3 trials are multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo and ustekinumab-controlled evaluations of the proposed dose and dosing regimen (risankizumab 150 mg SC at Weeks 0, 4, and q12w thereafter) in adults with stable moderate to severe chronic plaque psoriasis \ge 6 months.
L1301: Approximately 1000 patients with moderate to severe psoriasis enrolled in the two phase 3 clinical trials. Patients, stratified by weight and prior TNFi-exposure, were randomized 3:1:1 to receive 150 mg risankizumab, 45/90 mg ustekinumab, or placebo (dosing at Weeks 0, 4, 16, 28, and 40; at Week 16, patients initially randomized to placebo began to receive risankizumab). The studies included multiple assessments of efficacy (PASI, sPGA) and PROs (PSS, DLQI, EQ-5D-5L, and HADs). The current analyses utilized data from Visit 2 (baseline) through Visit 6 (Week 16). For study entry, patients had to have moderate to severe chronic plaque psoriasis, defined as \ge10% Body Surface Area (BSA) involvement, PASI score \ge12 and sPGA score \ge3.
L1302: Eight hundred and twenty-two (N=822) participants were enrolled and completed Day 1 assessments. Subsequent measurements included Week 4, Week 8, Week 12 and Week 16 assessments. The average age was 47.3 years (SD=13.8 years) with majority of men in the sample (69.9%, n=574). Most of the sample was non-Hispanic or Latino (89.2%, n=732) with a

L1308: majority of white race (77.0%, n=632). About a fifth of the sample (n=183, 22.3%) had previous experience with non-TNF biologics. The mean duration of psoriasis diagnosis was 17.9 years (SD=12.4 years) and 84.4% of the sample (n=693) did not have psoriatic arthritis, as confirmed via CASPAR. The average weight of the participants was 90.1 Kgs (SD= 21.8).
L1309: This section provides a synopsis of the psychometric results from Studies M16-0008 and M15-995. The full psychometric findings are in the evidence dossier (Assessment of Other Measurement Properties section of the Evidence Dossier).
L1310: ## Results
L1311: ## - **Item-level analyses**
L1312: - Floor effects (&gt;25% of respondents endorsing the lowest possible score) were observed in all visits except Day 1 for the single day scores and average weekly scores. The maximum floor effect was seen in Item 4, at Week 16 with 68.2% (n=496) endorsing the lowest possible score in the PSS.
- Ceiling effects (&gt;25% of respondents endorsing the highest possible score) were not observed over the 16-week evaluation period for single day scores and average weekly scores.
- Item-item correlations and item-to-total correlations ranged from 0.48-0.93.
L1313: **Reviewer's comment(s):** The floor effects seen in other visits is not a concern as this is more likely due to patients being on active treatment. There were no floor effects observed on Day 1.
L1314: ## - **Reliability**
L1315: - Test-retest reliability:Test-retest reliability in stable patients ranged from 0.92 (Weeks 8–12) to 0.94 (Weeks 12–16) for the PSS total scores. For the PSS individual item scores, ICCs ranged from 0.85 to 0.93.
L1316: **Reviewer's comment(s):** The sPGA was used to identify stable patients. Patients who have the same sPGA score at the test-retest assessment time points (Weeks 8 and 12, and Weeks 12 and 16) were defined as stable. The test-retest reliability estimates fall within the acceptable range.
L1317: - Internal consistency reliability: Evidence that items comprising an instrument measure the same general concept.Cronbach's alpha ranged from 0.85-0.92 (Day 1 (\alpha=0.85), Week 8 (\alpha=0.91) and Week 16 (\alpha=0.92)).
L1318: **Reviewer's comment(s):** The internal consistency reliability estimates fall within the acceptable range.
L1319: ## - **Construct validity**
L1320: ## - Convergent and discriminant validity
L1321: - Moderate to weak associations were observed between PSS total scores and EQ-5D-5L at Day 1, Week 12 and Week 16. The PSS total score was negatively correlated with the index score (r=-0.44, p&lt;0.0001) and with the EQ-5D VAS (r=-0.36, p&lt;0.0001) at Day 1 and similar results were found at Week 12 and Week 16.
- Moderate to strong associations were observed between PSS total scores and DLQI at Day 1, Week 12 and Week 16, except for Work and School dimension at Week 12, which was correlated at r=0.27 (p&lt;0.0001). Individual PSS items were strongly correlated with Item 1 on DLQI and the DLQI total scores at all time points whereas, the individual items demonstrated a weak to moderate association with other individual dimensions of the DLQI.
- PSS total score was moderately associated with HADS total score at all time points (r=0.33, 0.38 and 0.40, p&lt;0.0001). Individual items demonstrated weak to moderate correlations for all time points.
L1322: **Reviewer's comment(s):** The association between PSS total scores and EQ-5D-5L, DLQI, and HADS were expected to be moderate to weak except the item 1 in DLQI as the concepts measured in EQ-5D-5L, DLQI (except item 1), and HADS are remotely related to the concept measured by PSS.
L1323: ## - Known groups validity
L1324: - Based on sPGA four group total scores at Week 12 and Week 16, the PSS demonstrated significant differentiation among severity levels of pain, redness, itching and burning (all values at p&lt;0.0001; F=265.6, p&lt;0.0001 at Week 12 and F=170.3, p&lt;0.0001 at Week 16). Similar results were found when the three group total scores of sPGA were evaluated. PSS demonstrated significant differences in severity levels of sPGA scores (all values at p&lt;0.0001; F=204.1, p&lt;0.0001 at Week 12 and F=244.0, p&lt;0.0001 at Week 16)
- Based on DLQI total scores at Week 12, the PSS demonstrated significant differentiation among severity levels of pain, redness, itching and burning (all values at p&lt;0.0001; F=265.6, p&lt;0.0001 for the overall model). Similarly, at Week 16, the PSS was able to significantly differentiate amongst the total DLQI scores of 0-1 and \ge 2 (overall F=384.5, p&lt;0.0001).
- The PSS also demonstrated significant differentiation among severity levels for the PASI at Week 12 and Week 16 (F=206.3 for Week 12 and F=173.0 for Week 16, p&lt;0.0001 for overall model)
L1325: ## Clinical Outcome Assessment Review
L1326: Julia Ju, PharmD., PhD.
L1327: BLA 761105
L1328: Risankizumab
L1329: PSS, severity of psoriasis symptoms
L1330: **Reviewer's comment(s):** The results from the known groups analyses appears to demonstrate that the PSS can distinguish among different disease severities. Note that sPGA and PASI may be more relevant outcomes to classify psoriasis disease severity versus DLQI. Also, it is unclear whether the cut-offs chosen or the sPGA scores appropriately classify the psoriasis disease severity; however, the scores for the PSS show a monotonic increase for each category suggesting that the PSS can distinguish between different psoriasis severity levels.
L1331: ## - **Ability to detect change**
L1332: - Mean change from baseline to Week 12 on PSS total score was -5.9 (0.1) for responders and -0.3 (0.2) for non-responders on the PASI \ge50% (F=651.7; p&lt;0.0001). Significant differences in PSS change scores among PASI \ge75%, PASI \ge90% and PASI 100% responder and non-responder groups were also noted (all p&lt;0.0001). Similar pattern of results was found for mean change from baseline to Week 16 on the PSS total scores (all p&lt;0.0001) for responders and non-responder groups on PASI \ge50%, PASI \ge75%, PASI \ge90% and PASI 100%.
- Mean change from baseline to Week 12 on PSS total score was -6.2 (0.1) for responders and -2.3 (0.2) for non-responders based on the sPGA (F=488.4; p&lt;0.0001). Similar pattern of results was found for mean change from baseline to Week 16 on the PSS total scores (all p&lt;0.0001) for responder and non-responder groups on sPGA (F=542.2 (p&lt;0.0001)).
L1333: **Reviewer's comment(s):** The PSS appears to be able to detect change in those who respond to treatment effect.
L1334: ## 7 INTERPRETATION OF SCORES
L1335: The Applicant used anchor-based methods to help interpret the score changes in the PSS. The PASI and sPGA were used as anchors.
L1336: **Reviewer's comment(s):** The general recommendation is to use multiple anchor scales to provide an accumulation of evidence to help interpret a clinically meaningful within-patient score change in the PSS from the patient perspective. While the Applicant used the PASI and sPGA as anchors, these are clinician-reported anchors. Note that patient-reported anchors are more suitable to assess within-patient meaningful change in a PRO instrument.
L1337: Using the PASI improvement status at Week 16 to derive the threshold for within-patient meaningful change in the PSS total score, a meaningful score change on PSS ranges from \sim 5.1–\sim 6.7 points anchored to PASI 50–75% and PASI \ge 90\%, respectively. PASI 50–75% reflects that 50–75% of the disease is resolved. PASI \ge 90\% reflects that \ge 90\% of the disease is resolved. Clinical tends to focus on PASI90 and PASI 100 when evaluating disease improvement.
L1338: ## Clinical Outcome Assessment Review
L1339: Julia Ju, PharmD., PhD.
L1340: BLA 761105
L1341: Risankizumab
L1342: PSS, severity of psoriasis symptoms
L1343: *Using the sPGA improvement status as an anchor at Week 16, a meaningful score change on the PSS ranges from ~3.7 -6.3 anchored to a 1-point and 2-point improvement on the sPGA, respectively.*
L1344: *The Applicant provided an empirical cumulative distribution function (eCDF) curve; however, it was not an anchor-based eCDF curve and doesn't provide any meaningful information.*
L1345: *Anchor-based eCDF curves were not requested from the Applicant since the key secondary endpoint was based on the proportion of patients achieving a score of 0 (resolution of psoriasis signs/symptoms) which is indicative of clinical benefit.*
L1346: ## 8 LANGUAGE TRANSLATION AND CULTURAL ADAPTATION
L1347: The PSS was fully linguistically translated and validated based on the FACIT methodology (two forward translations, one reconciled version of the two forward translations, one back-translation into English, independent review, harmonization across languages) to 15 other languages besides English. FACIT trans conducted cognitive interviews to assess the clarity, interpretation, and appropriateness of the questionnaire content. Qualitative analyses of patients' comments confirmed the linguistic equivalence of each translated version.
L1348: *Reviewer's comment(s): The method used for language translation and cultural adaptation appears to be appropriate and consistent with ISPOR best practices.*

L1370: Line graph showing the Proportion of Subjects scoring Clear (Y-axis, 0 to 100) versus Weeks (X-axis, 0 to 52) for Trial 1311.3 (sPGA 0 or 1).
The graph compares three treatment groups:
Risankizumab (solid line, circles)
Ustekinumab (dashed line, triangles)
Placebo-Risankizumab (dotted line, crosses)
At Week 0, all groups start at 0% clear. By Week 16, Risankizumab reaches approximately 85% clear, while Ustekinumab reaches approximately 65% clear, and Placebo-Risankizumab reaches approximately 10% clear. Risankizumab maintains the highest proportion of clear subjects throughout the trial, peaking around 85% at Week 34 and remaining above 80% until Week 52. Ustekinumab peaks around 68% at Week 22 and ends around 58% at Week 52. Placebo-Risankizumab peaks around 80% at Week 34 and ends around 55% at Week 52.

L1372: Diagram illustrating the trial design for NDA/BLA 761105 (Skyrizi, Risankizumab).
The trial is divided into two parallel arms, each starting with N=300 subjects.
Arm 1 (N=300): BI 655066 150mg at weeks 0, 4, 16, 20 (Pbo), 28, 32 (Pbo).
Arm 2 (N=300): Adalimumab 80mg at randomisation, then 40mg at weeks 1, 3, 5, 7, 9, 11, 13, 15.
Subjects in Arm 2 are re-randomized at Week 16 based on their PASI score:
If PASI <50: BI 655066 150mg at weeks 16, 20, 28 (Pbo), 32.
If PASI 50 to <90: BI 655066 150mg at weeks 16, 20, 28 (Pbo), 32.
If PASI \ge 90: Continue on Adalimumab 40mg at weeks 17, 19, 21, 23, 25, 27, 29, 31, 33, 35, 37, 39, 41.
The timeline shows study visits (I) at V1, Rand, Wk4, Wk8, Wk12, Wk16, Wk16 (re-randomization), Wk20, Wk24, Wk28, Wk32, Wk36, Wk40, Wk44, and E00.
Legend:
↓ = BI 655066 (active or placebo) dosing. (2 syringes)
↓ = Adalimumab (active or placebo) dosing. (1 syringe)
I = Study Visit
L1373: Figure 9 is a flowchart illustrating the Study Design for Trial 1311.4, divided into Part A (Weeks 0 to 16) and Part B (Weeks 16 to 104). The study began with Randoization (N=500, 4:1 ratio).
Part A (Weeks 0 to 16):
Blinded BI 655066 150 mg (N=400) and Blinded Placebo (n=100).
At Week 16, subjects were evaluated.
Part B (Weeks 16 to 104):
Group 1 (Blinded BI 655066 150 mg, n=400):
At Week 28, subjects were evaluated (sPGA 0 or 1).
If sPGA \ge 2, they received Open label BI 655066 150 mg Q12W until Week 88.
If sPGA 0 or 1, they continued Blinded BI 655066 150 mg Q12W.
If sPGA \ge 3 (Week 32+), they were rescued to Open label BI 655066 150 mg Q12W.
Group 2 (Blinded Placebo, n=100):
At Week 28, subjects were evaluated (sPGA 0 or 1).
If sPGA \ge 2, they received Open label BI 655066 150 mg Q12W until Week 88.
If sPGA 0 or 1, they continued Blinded BI 655066 150 mg Q12W.
If sPGA \ge 3 (Week 32+), they were rescued to Open label BI 655066 150 mg Q12W.
The study concluded with a 16 week follow up (Weeks 94 to 104).

L1386: Figure 20 displays three plots showing the Observed versus Predicted Exposure-Response Relationship for Efficacy, comparing Response Rate (%) on the Y-axis against Risankizumab Cavg (\mu\text{g/mL}) week 16 on the X-axis. The plots are for PASI75, PASI90, and PASI100.
In all three plots, the solid line represents the logistic regression of the predicted response. The yellow shaded area represents the 95% Confidence Interval (CI) for the predicted response. Observed response rates are plotted as circles with error bars (95% CI) corresponding to the mean concentration of each exposure decile.
The blue bar below the X-axis indicates the 5% to 95% quantile of exposure of risankizumab at the proposed dosing regimen of 150mg. The bar is segmented into two groups based on body weight (BW):
150mg BW>100kg (n=375)
150mg BW\le100kg (n=931)
The plots show that for PASI75 and PASI90, the observed response rates are generally stable and high (around 0.75 to 0.85) across the exposure range. For PASI100, the observed response rate increases steadily with increasing exposure, reaching approximately 0.75 at the highest exposure level shown.
